From Hitchin Systems to Rational Elliptic Surfaces with ℂ ∗ \mathbb{C}^{*} -actions via Orbifold Hilbert Schemes
Yonghong Huang

Abstract
Using orbifold Hilbert schemes, we compactify all two-dimensional Hitchin systems corresponding to A ~ 0 \widetilde{A}_{0} , D ~ 4 \widetilde{D}_{4} , E ~ 6 \widetilde{E}_{6} , E ~ 7 \widetilde{E}_{7} , and E ~ 8 \widetilde{E}_{8} , thereby obtaining four rational elliptic surfaces with ℂ ∗ \mathbb{C}^{*} -actions. Their singular fibers and relative minimal models are listed in Table 1 . To this end, we prove that Hilbert schemes of orbifold surfaces are connected smooth projective schemes under suitable conditions, and we use the Hilbert–Chow morphism to construct the minimal resolutions of the coarse moduli spaces.



1
Introduction

The study of Higgs bundles originates in Hitchin’s work on the two-dimensional reduction of the self-dual Yang–Mills equations
[
Hi87
]
. Boalch conjectured that the Hilbert scheme of
n
points on a two-dimensional moduli space of meromorphic Higgs bundles is itself a moduli space of meromorphic Higgs bundles
[
Boa11
]
. This was confirmed by Gorsky, Nekrasov, and Rubtsov, who showed that the Hilbert schemes of points on cotangent bundles of elliptic curves are moduli spaces of parabolic Higgs bundles
[
GNR01
]
.

Building on this, Groechenig constructed all two-dimensional Hitchin systems associated with the affine Dynkin diagrams
\widetilde{A}_{0}
,
\widetilde{D}_{4}
,
\widetilde{E}_{6}
,
\widetilde{E}_{7}
, and
\widetilde{E}_{8}
via crepant resolutions of the GIT quotients
T^{\vee}E/\Gamma
, where
E
is an elliptic curve with a finite cyclic group action
[
Go14
]
. In the same work, he proved Boalch’s conjecture.

The goal of this paper is to provide explicit descriptions of these two-dimensional Hitchin systems. To this end, we study their compactifications into algebraic surfaces using Hilbert schemes, and we explain the rationale behind this approach.

For a projective normal surface
X
, there exists the canonical stack
X^{\mathrm{can}}
with coarse moduli space
X
. If
X
is the quotient of a smooth projective surface
M
by a finite group
G
, and its singularities are rational double points, Bridgeland, King, and Reid constructed the minimal resolution via Hilbert schemes on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
, thereby proving the derived McKay correspondence, although not using this terminology
[
BKR01
]
. For general quotient singularities, Ishii proved that
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}([\mathbb{C}^{2}/G])
is the minimal resolution of
\mathbb{C}^{2}/G

[
Is02
]
.

1.1
Main results

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an orbifold surface with stacky locus of codimension two, and let
\pi:\mathcal{X}\to X
denote its coarse moduli space. In Section
2
, we study changes of polarization on
\mathcal{X}
and show that there exists a polarization
H
on
X
such that, for a generic numerical
K
-class
\upsilon\in K^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathcal{X})
, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
of semistable sheaves contains no strictly semistable objects (see Theorem
2.18
and Lemma
3.3
). Assuming either
K_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot\pi^{*}H<0
or
K_{\mathcal{X}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
, we prove that
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
is a connected smooth projective scheme (Proposition
3.4
, Corollary
4.3
). In particular, if
\mathcal{X}
admits a Poisson structure, then
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
is a connected smooth projective scheme equipped with a natural Poisson structure (Proposition
3.11
). To establish this, we construct the Atiyah class on smooth Deligne–Mumford stacks (Section
3.1
), which differs from the general construction for algebraic stacks given by Kuhn
[
Ku24
]
. Using the Atiyah class, we then define the Kodaira–Spencer map for
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
, and thereby obtain explicit descriptions of its tangent and cotangent bundles (Proposition
3.5
, Corollary
3.6
). Moreover, we represent the diagonal class of
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
in terms of Chern classes, and show that
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
is generated by the Künneth components of the orbifold Chern character of a universal sheaf (Theorem
4.1
). In Section
5
, we construct the Hilbert scheme
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
for
\alpha\in\mathcal{N}
(notation in Corollary
5.5
), prove its smoothness, and compute its dimension (Proposition
5.6
). The key point we obtain is

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume that
K_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot H<0
or
K_{\mathcal{X}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

(1)

For any
\alpha\in\mathcal{N}
,
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
is a smooth connected projective scheme. Furthermore, if
\mathcal{X}
admits a Poisson structure, then
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
admits one as well.

(2)

The Hilbert-Chow morphism
h:\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathop{\rm Sym}\nolimits^{n}(X)
is a resolution of singularities. If
\mathcal{X}
equipped with a Poisson structure, then
h
is a Poisson resolution with respect to the induced Poisson structures.

(3)

h:\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow X
is the minimal resolution.

∎

As a byproduct, we obtain the following proposition.

Proposition 1.2
.

\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}([\mathbb{C}^{2}/G])
is connected if
G
is a finite small group.
∎

When
G
is abelian, Bejleri and Zaimi have proved that
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}([\mathbb{C}^{2}/G])
is connected (see
[
BZ
, Corollary 2.3]
). For
G\subset\mathop{\textbf{\rm SL}}\nolimits_{2}
,
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}([\mathbb{C}^{2}/G])
is connected (see Ibid., footnote on page 2).

In Section
6
, we compactify the two-dimensional Hitchin systems via orbifold Hilbert schemes. Precisely, the two-dimensional Hitchin systems corresponding to the affine Dynkin diagrams
\widetilde{A}_{0}
,
\widetilde{D}_{4}
,
\widetilde{E}_{6}
,
\widetilde{E}_{7}
and
\widetilde{E}_{8}
are the moduli spaces of orbifold Higgs bundles on

E,\quad\mathds{P}_{2,2,2,2}^{1},\quad\mathds{P}_{3,3,3}^{1},\quad\mathds{P}_{4,4,2}^{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathds{P}_{6,3,2}^{1}

respectively, where
E
is an elliptic curve and
\mathds{P}^{1}_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{s}}
denotes a projective line with
s
orbifold points of the specified orders. These are precisely the one-dimensional Calabi-Yau orbifolds, and, except for
E
, each can be realized as a nontrivial quotient stack
\mathcal{X}_{i}:=[E_{i}/\mu_{i}]
of a certain elliptic curve
E_{i}
by cyclic group
\mu_{i}
for
i=2,3,4,6
. In the case of
\widetilde{A}_{0}
, the Higgs moduli space is
T^{\vee}E
, and its natural compactification is
\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}E\oplus\mathcal{O}_{E})
. Therefore we restrict our attention to the remaining cases.

Theorem 1.3
.

The two-dimensional Hitchin systems for
\widetilde{D}_{4}
,
\widetilde{E}_{6}
,
\widetilde{E}_{7}
, and
\widetilde{E}_{8}
admit natural compactifications

(1.1)

\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}\!\bigl(\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{i}})\bigr)

with the following properties:

(1)

The natural
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action and Poisson structure on
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{i}}))
are compatible with, and extend, the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action and symplectic structure on
\mathcal{M}(i)
.

(2)

The Hitchin maps extend to the compositions

(1.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}\!\bigl(\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{i}})\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\times\mathds{P}^{1}/\mu_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{P}^{1}/\mu_{i}\cong\mathds{P}^{1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}

where
h_{i}
are the Hilbert–Chow morphisms.

(3)

Each
h_{i}
is the minimal resolution of the GIT quotient

(1.3)

\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}E_{i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{E_{i}})/\mu_{i},

and provides a Poisson resolution.

(4)

The boundary (with reduced structure) consists of
s+1
copies of
\mathds{P}^{1}
, where
s
is the number of orbifold points of
\mathcal{X}_{i}
.

∎

For brevity, we denote the GIT quotient (
1.3
) and its compactifications (
1.1
) by
X_{i}
and
\widetilde{X}_{i}
, respectively.

Theorem 1.4
.

Each
\widetilde{X}_{i}
is a rational elliptic surface with a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action, whose fibration
\pi_{i}:\widetilde{X}_{i}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
has singular fibers only over
0
and
\infty
, as summarized in Table
1
. Each
X_{i}
is obtained by blowing up the second Hirzebruch surface (see Propositions
6.8
,
6.11
,
6.14
,
6.17
), and the Hitchin system
M(i)
is isomorphic to
\widetilde{X}_{i}
with the fiber over
\infty
removed.
∎

1.2
Notations and conventions

All schemes and Deligne-Mumford stacks are defined over
\mathbb{C}
. Deligne-Mumford stacks are assumed to be of finite type over
\mathbb{C}
with finite diagonal. Unless stated otherwise,
\mathcal{X}
denotes an irreducible orbifold surface with coarse moduli space
\pi:\mathcal{X}\to X
, whose stacky locus has codimension two. We write
K_{0}(\mathcal{X})_{\mathbb{C}}:=K_{0}(\mathcal{X})\otimes\mathbb{C}
for the Grothendieck group of coherent sheaves,
T\mathcal{X}
for the tangent sheaf, and
K_{\mathcal{X}}
for the canonical line bundle when
\mathcal{X}
is smooth. The category of quasicoherent sheaves is
\rm Qcoh
, and
\mathcal{X}_{\text{\'{e}t}}
denotes its small étale site. For Deligne-Mumford stacks
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, let
\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}
and
\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}
be the natural projections from
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
. For a locally free sheaf
E
on
\mathcal{X}
, the projective bundle
\mathds{P}(E)
is
{\bm{\mathop{\rm Proj}\nolimits}}(\rm Sym^{\bullet}E^{\vee})
.

Some basic facts on finite group representations: let
G
be a finite group and
K(G)
its representation ring over
\mathbb{Z}
. Enumerating the irreducible complex representations as
\{\rho_{0},\ldots,\rho_{t}\}
with
\rho_{0}
trivial, we have
K(G)=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{t}\mathbb{Z}\rho_{i}
. The character
\chi_{\rho}
of a representation
\rho
is
\chi_{\rho}(g)=\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\rho(g))
, and the inner product of two characters is

\langle\chi_{\rho},\chi_{\varrho}\rangle=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\rho(g))\overline{\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\varrho(g))}.

In particular, the first orthogonality relation reads
\langle\chi_{\rho_{i}},\chi_{\rho_{j}}\rangle=\delta_{ij}
.

2
Néron-Severi group and change of polarization

In this section we will study the change of polarization for
\mathcal{X}
and formulate some general results that will be needed later on. For a first reading, this section may be skipped and consulted later as needed.

2.1
Néron-Severi group of
\mathcal{X}

Recall the Picard functor

\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits_{\mathcal{X}}:\mathop{\mathbf{Sch}}\nolimits\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathbf{Sets}}\nolimits,\quad T\longmapsto\frac{\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X}\times T)}{\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(T)}.

is representable by a scheme
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})
and its connected component of identity
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X})
is an abelian varity of dimension
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{\mathbb{C}}H^{1}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})
(see
[
Br09
]
and
[
Br12
]
).

Definition 2.1
.

The Néron-Severi group of
\mathcal{X}
is defined as

\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X}):=\frac{\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})(\mathbb{C})}{\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X})(\mathbb{C})}.

Theorem 2.2

(Brochard)
.

The group
\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})
is of finite type. Moreover, if we view
\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})
as a constant group scheme, then we have the following exact sequence

∎

For
\mathcal{X}
, the associated analytic Deligne–Mumford stack
\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}
is defined in
[
Toe99(2)
, Définition 5.2]
.
The analytification functor

\mathop{\mathbf{\rm Coh}}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})\to\mathop{\mathbf{\rm Coh}}\nolimits(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}),\quad F\mapsto F^{\rm an}

is an equivalence of abelian categories (GAGA for DM stacks,
[
Toe99(2)
, Théorème 5.10]
), hence
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})\cong\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})
.
Moreover, by
[
Gr60
, proof of Théorème 3.3]
, the exponential sequence

(2.1)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X^{\rm an}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

holds for
\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}
. The associated long exact sequence is

(2.2)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise where
\mathop{\rm c}\nolimits_{1}(L)
is the first Chern class of
L\in\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})
.

Definition 2.3
.

The Néron-Severi group of
\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}
is

\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}):=\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})/\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})

where
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})
is
\mathop{\rm Ker}\nolimits(c_{1})\cong H^{1}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}})/H^{1}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an},\underline{\mathbb{Z}})
.

Remark 2.4
.

\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})
is a compact complex torus of dimension
b_{3}/2
, where
b_{3}
is the third Betti number of
X
(see (i), (ii) and (v) in Section
B
).

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\mathop{\mathbf{An}}\nolimits
be the category of analytic spaces. We consider the functor

(2.3)

\underline{\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits}_{\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}}:\mathop{\mathbf{An}}\nolimits\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathbf{Sets}}\nolimits,\quad S\longmapsto H^{0}(S,R^{1}p_{S*}\mathcal{O}^{*}_{\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}\times S})

where
p_{S}:\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}\times S\rightarrow S
is the projection to
S
.

Remark 2.6
.

If
\mathop{\mathbf{An}}\nolimits
is equipped with the usual analytic topology, then
\underline{\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits}_{\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}}
is the sheaf associated to the presheaf

(2.4)

\mathop{\mathbf{An}}\nolimits\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathbf{Sets}}\nolimits,\quad S\longmapsto\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}\times S).

Proposition 2.7
.

The functor
\underline{\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits}_{\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}}
is represented by the abelian analytic group
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})
, fitting into the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})\longrightarrow 0,

where
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})
is a compact complex torus of dimension
b_{3}/2
, and
\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})
is discrete.

Proof.

The proof of this proposition is the same as the proof of Théorème 3.3 in
[
Gr60
]
.
∎

Corollary 2.8
.

As analytic groups,
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})^{\rm an}\cong\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})
and
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X})^{\rm an}\cong\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be the Poincaré line bundle on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})
.
Its analytification
\mathcal{P}^{\rm an}
induces a morphism of analytic groups
a:\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})^{\rm an}\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})
. Since
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X})
is an abelian variety
[
Br09
, Théorème 1.3]
,

\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X})^{\rm an}
is a compact complex torus
[
SGA1
, Proposition 3.2, Exposé XII]
,
and it is the identity component of
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})^{\rm an}

[
SGA1
, Corollaire 2.6, Exposé XII]
. Restricting
a
gives
a^{\prime}:\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X})^{\rm an}\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})
, a holomorphic injection between compact complex tori of dimension
b_{3}/2
. Hence
a^{\prime}
is an isomorphism
[
GH94
, p. 19]
, and therefore
a
is an isomorphism of analytic groups. ∎

Corollary 2.9
.

\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})\cong\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})
.
∎

Proposition 2.10
.

\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X})
is isomorphic to
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(X)
as group schemes.

Proof.

The coarse moduli space of
\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}
is
X^{\rm an}
, the analytification of
X
(see
[
Toe99(2)
]
). Since
\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}=\underline{\mathbb{Z}}
,
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}}=\mathcal{O}_{X^{\rm an}}
and
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}}^{*}=\mathcal{O}_{X^{\rm an}}^{*}
, we have the following diagram where horizontal and vertical sequences are exact.

0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 176.96791pt\raise-97.07225pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 176.96791pt\raise-97.07225pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 182.46791pt\raise-73.28003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 250.79012pt\raise-97.07225pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 250.79012pt\raise-97.07225pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 256.29012pt\raise-73.28003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 324.61232pt\raise-97.07225pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 324.61232pt\raise-97.07225pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 330.11232pt\raise-73.28003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

We use the following facts.

(1)

For any abelian sheaf
J
on
\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}
, the hypercohomology groups
\mathbb{H}^{i}(X^{\rm an},\mathop{\rm R}\nolimits\pi_{*}J)\cong H^{i}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an},J)
.

(2)

For
j>0
, the support of
\mathop{\rm R}\nolimits^{j}\pi_{*}J
lies in the stacky locus. The stalk at a stacky point
p_{k}
is
H^{j}(G_{k},J_{q_{k}})
, where
J_{q_{k}}
is the stalk of
J
at a lift
q_{k}
of
p_{k}
.
Hence

\mathbb{H}^{i}(X^{\rm an},\mathop{\rm R}\nolimits\pi_{*}J/J)=\begin{cases}0&i=0,\\
\bigoplus_{k}H^{i}(G_{k},J_{q_{k}})&i>0.\end{cases}

(3)

In particular,

\mathbb{H}^{i}(X^{\rm an},\mathop{\rm R}\nolimits\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}/\underline{\mathbb{Z}})=\begin{cases}0&i=0,1,\\
\bigoplus_{k}H^{i}(G_{k},\mathbb{Z})&i\geq 2,\end{cases}

using
H^{1}(G_{k},\mathbb{Z})=0
.

(4)

Since
\mathop{\rm R}\nolimits^{i}\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
for
i=0
and vanishes for
i>1

[
Ni08
, Remark 1.4(3)]
, the same holds for
\pi:\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}\to X^{\rm an}

[
Toe99(2)
, GAGA Theorem]
.

Chasing the diagram then gives
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(X^{\rm an})\cong\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})
, hence
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(X)^{\rm an}\cong\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X})^{\rm an}
(Proposition
2.8
). Since the functor of analytification on proper schemes is fully faithful (see
[
SGA1
, Corollaire 4.5 in exposé XII]
), we conclude
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(X)\cong\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X})
.
∎

Corollary 2.11
.

The Néron–Severi groups satisfy:

(1)

There is a short exact sequence

(2.7)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})}/{\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where
\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})/\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(X)
is finite.

(2)

The Picard numbers agree:
\rho(\mathcal{X})=\rho(X)
.

Proof.

By diagram
2.6
, we obtain

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.72636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 19.72636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.85553pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 69.85553pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 118.4153pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 118.4153pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigoplus_{k}H^{2}(G_{k},\mathbb{Z})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Since each
H^{2}(G_{k},\mathbb{Z})
is finite, the claim follows.
∎

Proposition 2.12
.

(1)

The torsion part
\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})_{\rm tor}
of
\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})
is isomorphic to the torsion part
H^{2}(\mathcal{X},\underline{\mathbb{Z}})_{\rm tor}
of
H^{2}(\mathcal{X},\underline{\mathbb{Z}})
.

(2)

If we use
\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{X})
to indicate
\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
, then we have

(2.8)

\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{X})=H^{1,1}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})\cap H^{2}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an},\underline{\mathbb{R}}).

Proof.

By the torsion freeness of
H^{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})
, we have
\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})_{\rm tor}=H^{2}(\mathcal{X},\underline{\mathbb{Z}})_{\rm tor}
, and hence

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0359pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0359pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})}/{\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})_{\rm tor}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 100.9748pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 100.9748pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{H^{2}(\mathcal{X},\underline{\mathbb{Z}})}/{H^{2}(\mathcal{X},\underline{\mathbb{Z}})}_{\rm tor}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 204.5803pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 204.5803pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H^{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Tensoring with
\mathbb{R}
gives the exact sequence

(2.9)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(\mathcal{X},\underline{\mathbb{R}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})}

The last arrow in (
2.9
) is isomorphic to the composition

(2.10)

\begin{split}H^{2}(\mathcal{X},\underline{\mathbb{R}})\hookrightarrow H^{2}(\mathcal{X},\underline{\mathbb{C}})=H^{2,0}(\mathcal{X})\oplus H^{1,1}(\mathcal{X})\oplus H^{0,2}(\mathcal{X})\xrightarrow{\rm pr}H^{0,2}(\mathcal{X})\end{split}

using Dolbeaut-Kodaira and Hodge decomposition (see (iv), (v) in Section
B
). Thus
\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{X})\cong H^{1,1}(\mathcal{X})\cap H^{2}(\mathcal{X},\underline{\mathbb{R}})
.

∎

2.2
Change of Polarization

Following the case of smooth projective surfaces (see
[
HL10
, Section 4.C]
), we will prove some basic facts about the change of polarization for
\mathcal{X}
.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\mathop{\rm Num}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})
be the torsion free part of
\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})
, i.e.
\mathop{\rm Num}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})=\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})/\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})_{\rm tor}
.

Lemma 2.14
.

(1)

\mathop{\rm Num}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})
is a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module of rank
\rho(\mathcal{X})
.

(2)

\mathop{\rm Num}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{X})\cong\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{X})
, where
\mathop{\rm Num}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{X}):=\mathop{\rm Num}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
.

(3)

\mathop{\rm Num}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{X})\cong\mathop{\rm Num}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
.

(4)

The positive cone is defined as

K^{+}(\mathcal{X}):=\{x\in\mathop{\rm Num}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{X})|x^{2}>0\text{ and $x\cdot H>0$ for some ample divisor $H$}\}.

Then the cone
A(\mathcal{X})
spanned by the ample divisors is an open subcone of
K^{+}(\mathcal{X})
.

Proof.

By Corollary
2.11
, the statements (1), (2), (3) are immediate. First, note that
K^{+}(\mathcal{X})\cong K^{+}(X)
and
A(\mathcal{X})\cong A(X)
. Recall that
A(X)
is an open subcone of
K^{+}(X)
. Then the result is also true for
\mathcal{X}
.
∎

Definition 2.15
.

If we fix a generating sheaf
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{X}
, then a polarization of
\mathcal{X}
is a ray
\mathbb{R}_{>0}\cdot H
, where
H\in A(\mathcal{X})
.

By the Hodge index Theorem,
\mathop{\rm Num}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{X})
carries a Minkowski metric. For any
u\in\mathop{\rm Num}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{X})
, let
|u|=|u^{2}|^{1/2}
. The set
\mathbf{H}
of rays in
K^{+}
can be identified with the hyperbolic manifold
\{H\in K^{+}\mid|H|=1\}
. The hyperbolic metric
d_{\mathbf{H}}
is defined as

d_{\mathbf{H}}(H,H^{\prime}):=\mathop{\rm arccosh}\nolimits\left(\frac{H\cdot H^{\prime}}{|H|\cdot|H^{\prime}|}\right)

for
H,H^{\prime}\in\mathbf{H}
.

Definition 2.16
.

Let
r\geq 2
be an integer and let
\Delta
be a positive rational number. Then
\xi\in\mathop{\rm Num}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})
is said to be of type
(r,\Delta)
if

-\frac{r^{2}}{4}\Delta\leq\xi^{2}<0.

The wall defined by
\xi
is the hyperplane
W_{\xi}:=\{H\in\mathbf{H}\mid\xi\cdot H=0\}
.

The locally finiteness of walls is also holds for
\mathcal{X}
and the proof does not differ from the usual case.

Lemma 2.17
.

The set of walls of type
(r,\Delta)
is locally finite in
\mathbf{H}
, i.e. for every point
H\in\mathbf{H}
, there exists an open neighborhood of
H
intersecting only finitely many walls of type
(r,\Delta)
.
∎

Fix an ample divisor
H
on the coarse moduli space
X
. We can introduce the notion of
\mu_{H}
-stability for torsion free sheaves on
\mathcal{X}
. Suppose that
E
is a torsion free sheaf on
\mathcal{X}
. The
\mu_{H}
-slope of
E
is

(2.11)

\mu_{H}(E)=\frac{\mathop{\rm c}\nolimits_{1}(E)\cdot\pi^{*}H}{\mathop{\rm rk}\nolimits(E)}

The discriminant of
E
is defined as

(2.12)

\Delta(E)=2\mathop{\rm rk}\nolimits(E)\mathop{\rm c}\nolimits_{2}(E)-(\mathop{\rm rk}\nolimits(E)-1)^{2}\mathop{\rm c}\nolimits_{1}(E).

Theorem 2.18
.

Suppose that
F
is a
\mu_{H}
-semistable sheaf of rank
r
and discriminant
\Delta
on
\mathcal{X}
.

(1)

If
F^{\prime}
is a rank
r^{\prime}
coherent subsheaf of
F
such that

0<r^{\prime}<r
and
\mu_{H}(F^{\prime})=\mu_{H}(F)
, then
\xi=r\cdot\mathop{\rm c}\nolimits_{1}(F^{\prime})-r^{\prime}\cdot\mathop{\rm c}\nolimits_{1}(F)
satisfies

\xi\cdot\pi^{*}H=0\quad\text{and}\quad-\frac{r^{2}}{4}\Delta(F)\leq\xi^{2}\leq 0

where
\xi^{2}=0
if and only if
\xi=0
.

(2)

If
\mathop{\rm c}\nolimits_{1}(F)\in\mathop{\rm Num}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})
is indivisible, then there exists an ample line bundle
H
on
X
such that the
\mu_{H}
-semistableness implies the
\mu_{H}
-stableness.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
F^{\prime}
is saturated, i.e.
F^{\prime\prime}:=F/F^{\prime}
is torsion free. Note that the following identity

(2.13)

\Delta(F)=\frac{r}{r^{\prime}}\Delta(F^{\prime})+\frac{r}{r^{\prime\prime}}\Delta(F)-\frac{\xi^{2}}{r^{\prime}r^{\prime\prime}}

holds, where
r^{\prime\prime}
is the rank of
F^{\prime\prime}
. By the Bogomolov inequality of the stacky version (see
[
Li11
, Proposition 4.2.4]
, we get

(2.14)

-\xi^{2}\leq r^{\prime}r^{\prime\prime}\Delta(F)\leq\frac{r^{2}}{4}\Delta(F).

Since
\xi\cdot\pi^{*}H=0
, it derived from the Hodge index Theorem
B.1
that
\xi^{2}\leq 0
with equality if and only if
\xi=0
. If
\mathop{\rm c}\nolimits_{1}(F)\in\mathop{\rm Num}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})
is not divisible, then
\xi
is not zero in
\mathop{\rm Num}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})
. By Lemma
2.17
, we can find an ample divisor
H
such that there do not exist strictly
\mu_{H}
-semitable sheaves with rank
r
and discriminant
\Delta
.
∎

3
The Poisson structure on the moduli spaces of stable sheaves

3.1
The Atiyah class

We construct the Atiyah class on smooth projective Deligne-Mumford stacks (for the general case, see
[
Ku24
]
). To begin, recall the construction of Atiyah class for schemes (see
[
Il71
]
for details). Let
S
be a separable scheme of finite type over
\mathbb{C}
, and let
E
be a coherent sheaf on
S
. Consider the exact sequence

(3.1)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(I_{S}/I^{2}_{S})\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{O}_{S\times S}/I^{2}_{S})\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits^{*}_{2}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{O}_{S\times S}/I_{S})\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where
I_{S}
is the ideal sheaf of the diagonal in
S\times S
. The pushforward of (
3.1
) along
\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}:S\times S\to S
yields the exact sequence

(3.2)

0\to E\otimes\Omega_{S}\to P^{1}_{S}(E)\to E\to 0,

where
P^{1}_{S}(E)=\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1*}\big((\mathcal{O}_{S\times S}/I_{S}^{2})\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}E\big)
. Its extension class
\mathop{\rm At}\nolimits(E)\in\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}(E,E\otimes\Omega_{S})
is the Atiyah class of
E
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose that
f:T\rightarrow S
is an étale morphism between separable schemes. Then there is a functorial isomorphism
f^{*}P^{1}_{S}(E)\longrightarrow P^{1}_{T}(f^{*}E)
. For another étale morphism
g:W\rightarrow T
between separable schemes, then

(f\circ g)^{*}P^{1}_{S}(E)\longrightarrow P^{1}_{W}((f\circ g)^{*}E))

is isomorphic to the composite

g^{*}f^{*}P^{1}_{S}(E)\rightarrow g^{*}P^{1}_{T}(f^{*}E)\rightarrow P^{1}_{W}(g^{*}f^{*}E)

up to canonical isomorphisms.

Proof.

Pulling (
3.1
) back to
T\times T
, we get

(3.3)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J_{T}/J^{2}_{T}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}f^{*}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{T\times T}/J^{2}_{T}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits^{*}_{2}f^{*}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{T\times T}/J_{T}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}f^{*}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where
J_{T}=(f\times f)^{*}I_{S}
is the ideal sheaf of
T\times_{S}T
in
T\times T
. Since the diagonal of
T\times T
is an open and closed component of
T\times_{S}T
, restricting (
3.3
) to the diagonal of
T\times T
, we get

(3.4)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I_{T}/I^{2}_{T}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}f^{*}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{T\times T}/I^{2}_{T}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits^{*}_{2}f^{*}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{T\times T}/I_{T}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}f^{*}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where
I_{T}
is the ideal sheaf of diagonal in
T\times T
. Then we have a functorial isomorphism of short exact sequences

(3.5)

\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise By the above construction of the functorial isomorphism, the second statement of the lemma is immediate.
∎

Suppose that
\mathcal{Y}
is a smooth projective Deligne-Mumford stack. Then there exists an étale cover
U\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
with
U
affine. Consider the cartesian diagram

\textstyle{U\times_{\mathcal{Y}}U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}}

For a coherent sheaf
F
on
\mathcal{Y}
, let
F[1]
denote its pullback to
U
. There is an isomorphism
\sigma:{\rm pr}_{1}^{*}E[1]\longrightarrow{\rm pr}_{2}^{*}E[1]
on
U\times_{\mathcal{Y}}U
satisfying the cocycle condition
{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits}_{23}^{*}\sigma\circ{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits}_{12}^{*}\sigma={\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits}_{13}^{*}\sigma
on
U\times_{\mathcal{Y}}U\times_{\mathcal{Y}}U
, where
\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}
,
\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}
and
\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}
are the natural projections from
U\times_{\mathcal{Y}}U\times_{\mathcal{Y}}U
to
U\times_{\mathcal{Y}}U
. Applying Lemma
3.1
to
\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}
and
\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}
, we get two canonical isomorphism

(3.6)

\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}^{*}P^{1}_{U}(F[1])\rightarrow P^{1}_{U\times_{\mathcal{Y}}U}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}^{*}F[1])\quad\text{and}\quad\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}P^{1}_{U}(F[1])\rightarrow P^{1}_{U\times_{\mathcal{Y}}U}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}F[1]).

In addition, we also have an isomorphism

(3.7)

P^{1}_{U\times_{\mathcal{Y}}U}(\sigma):P^{1}_{U\times_{\mathcal{Y}}U}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}^{*}F[1])\rightarrow P^{1}_{U\times_{\mathcal{Y}}U}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}F[1]).

Composing (
3.7
) with (
3.6
), we get an isomorphism
\widetilde{\sigma}:\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}^{*}P^{1}_{U}(F[1])\rightarrow\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}P^{1}_{U}(F[1])
. By the second statement of Lemma
3.1
,
\widetilde{\sigma}
satisfies the cocycle condition
\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\widetilde{\sigma}\circ\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}^{*}\widetilde{\sigma}=\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\widetilde{\sigma}
up to canonical isomorphisms. This gives the exact sequence

(3.8)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern Definition 3.2
.

The Atiyah class
\mathop{\rm At}\nolimits(F)\in\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{1}(F,F\otimes\Omega_{\mathcal{Y}})
of
F
is the extension class defined by (
3.8
).

Alternatively, consider the trivial square-zero extension of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}
by
\Omega_{\mathcal{Y}}

(3.9)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise Let
d_{\mathcal{Y}}:\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}\rightarrow\Omega_{\mathcal{Y}}
be the universal derivation of
\mathcal{Y}
. The morphism
(\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits,d_{\mathcal{Y}}):\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}\oplus\Omega_{\mathcal{Y}}
is a section of the projection in (
3.9
), giving
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}\oplus\Omega_{\mathcal{Y}}
a right
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}
-module structure. Tensoring with
F
yields an exact sequence

(3.10)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 19.72636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 19.72636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.8

3.2
The Smoothness of the moduli space of sheaves

Henceforth let
\mathcal{X}
be an orbifold surface with stacky locus of codimension two. Fix a numerical K-class
\upsilon\in K^{\rm num}(\mathcal{X})
with rank one or primitive first Chern class in
\mathop{\rm Num}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})
. By Theorem
2.18
, there exists an ample divisor
H
on
X
such that no strictly
\mu_{H}
-semistable sheaves of class
\upsilon
exist. Fix a generating sheaf
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{X}
(see
[
Ni08
, Definition 2.6]
), and let
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
denote the moduli space of Gieseker semistable torsion free sheaves of class
\upsilon
(see ibid.).

Lemma 3.3
.

\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
does not contain strictly semistable objects.

Proof.

Let
P_{E}(m)=\chi(E\otimes\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\otimes\pi^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}(nH))=a_{2}n^{2}/2+a_{1}n+a_{0}
be the modified Hilbert polynomial of
E
.
By Toën-Riemann-Roch formula (see
[
ts10
, Theorem A.0.6]
), we have

(3.11)

\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}=\frac{\mu_{H}(E)-\mu_{H}(\mathcal{E})-\mu_{H}(\Omega_{\mathcal{X}})}{H^{2}}.

If
E
is Gieseker semistable, then
E
is
\mu_{H}
-stable. Hence
E
is Gieseker stable.
∎

The determinant map
\mathop{\rm det}\nolimits:\mathcal{M}_{v}\rightarrow\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})
,
E\mapsto\mathop{\rm det}\nolimits(E)
; let
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}^{L}
be its fiber over
L\in\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits(\mathcal{X})
.

Proposition 3.4
.

If
K_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot H<0
or
K_{\mathcal{X}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
, then
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}^{L}
are smooth projective schemes.

Proof.

For a Gieseker stable sheaf
E
, both
E
and
E\otimes K_{\mathcal{X}}
are
\mu_{H}
-stable, so
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{\mathbb{C}}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(E,E\otimes K_{\mathcal{X}})\leq 1
. By Serre duality,
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}(E,E)_{0}\cong\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(E,E\otimes K_{\mathcal{X}})_{0}=0
. Then the standard argument in
[
HL10
, Theorm 4.5.4]
shows that
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}^{L}
are smooth.
∎

3.3
The Kodaira-Spencer map

Pick a (twisted) universal sheaf
\mathbf{E}
on
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}\times\mathcal{X}
. The decomposition
\Omega_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}\times\mathcal{X}}=\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}\oplus\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}
induces a decomposition of the Atiyah class
\mathop{\rm At}\nolimits(\mathbf{E})=\mathop{\rm At}\nolimits(\mathbf{E})_{1}+\mathop{\rm At}\nolimits(\mathbf{E})_{2}
, where
\mathop{\rm At}\nolimits(\mathbf{E})_{1}\in\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{1}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}})
and
\mathop{\rm At}\nolimits(\mathbf{E})_{2}\in\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{1}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{X}})
. Via the Grothendieck spectral sequence

E_{2}^{i,j}=H^{i}(\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon},\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{j}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}))\Longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{i+j}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}),

we get the global to local map
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{1}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits^{*}_{1}\Omega_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}})\rightarrow H^{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon},\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}))
, and we denote the image of
\mathop{\rm At}\nolimits(\mathbf{E})_{1}
by the same symbol. The Kodaira-Spencer map is then

{\rm KS}:T\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm At}\nolimits(\mathbf{E})_{1}}T{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}\otimes\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits^{*}_{1}\Omega_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}})\rightarrow\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}).

Proposition 3.5
.

\rm KS
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

For any closed point
p\in\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
and
\mathcal{v}\in T_{p}\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
, we have a morphism of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}
-modules
\mathcal{v}:\Omega_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, and its pullback
\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}^{*}\mathcal{v}:\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
. Consider the diagram

(3.12)

Corollary 3.6
.

The cotangent bundle of
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
is isomorphic to
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}^{1}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}K_{\mathcal{X}})
.

Proof.

By Grothendieck duality for Deligne-Mumford stacks (see
[
Ni09
, Corollary 2.10]
), we have that
R\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1*}R\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}K_{\mathcal{X}}[2])\cong R\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(R\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1*}R\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}),\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}})
. Taking cohomology gives
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}^{1}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}K_{\mathcal{X}})\cong\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}^{1}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}),\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}})
.

∎

3.4
The Poisson structure on
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}

Definition 3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a smooth Deligne-Mumford stack. A Poisson structure on
\mathcal{X}
is a bilinear operation
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
on
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
such that for any object
U\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathop{\text{\'{e}t}}\nolimits}
and any
f,g,h\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}(U\rightarrow\mathcal{X})
, the three axioms hold: (1) Skew-symmetry:
\{f,g\}=-\{g,f\}
; (2) Leibniz rule:
\{f,gh\}=\{f,g\}h+g\{f,h\}
; (3) Jacobi identity:
\{f,\{g,h\}\}+\{g,\{h,f\}\}+\{h,\{f,g\}\}=0
.

\{\cdot,\cdot\}
determines an antisymmetric contravariant
2
-tensor
\theta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{X},\wedge^{2}T_{\mathcal{X}})
by

(3.14)

\langle\theta,df\wedge dg\rangle:=\{f,g\}.

Conversely, any
\theta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{X},\wedge^{2}T_{\mathcal{X}})
defines a Poisson structure by (
3.14
) iff
\widetilde{d}\theta=0
(see
[
Bo95
, Proposition 1.1]
), where
\widetilde{d}:H^{0}(\mathcal{X},\wedge^{2}T_{\mathcal{X}})\rightarrow H^{0}(\mathcal{X},\wedge^{3}T_{\mathcal{X}})
is defined as follows: for any
1
-forms
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
,

(3.15)

\begin{split}\widetilde{d}\theta(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}):=B_{\theta}(\alpha_{1})\theta(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})+B_{\theta}(\alpha_{2})\theta(\alpha_{3},\alpha_{1})+B_{\theta}(\alpha_{3})\theta(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\\
-\langle[B_{\theta}(\alpha_{1}),B_{\theta}(\alpha_{2})],\alpha_{3}\rangle-\langle[B_{\theta}(\alpha_{2}),B_{\theta}(\alpha_{3})],\alpha_{1}\rangle-\langle[B_{\theta}(\alpha_{3}),B_{\theta}(\alpha_{1})],\alpha_{2}\rangle,\end{split}

where
B_{\theta}:\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow T_{\mathcal{X}}
is defined by
\langle\theta,\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2}\rangle=\langle B_{\theta}(\alpha_{1}),\alpha_{2}\rangle
and
[\cdot,\cdot]
is the usual commutator of the vector fields.

Lemma 3.8
.

If
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\mathcal{X}=2
, then Poisson structures on
\mathcal{X}
are global sections of
K_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}
.
∎

Definition 3.9
.

A symplectic structure on
\mathcal{X}
is a closed nondegenerate
2
-form on
\mathcal{X}
.

For a smooth Deligne-Mumford curve
\mathcal{C}
, the total space of
K_{\mathcal{C}}
is a symplectic, with natural compactification
\mathds{P}(K_{\mathcal{C}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{C}})
.

Proposition 3.10
.

\mathds{P}(K_{\mathcal{C}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{C}})
carries a Poisson structure extending the symplectic form on
K_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

From the Euler sequence one computes
\wedge^{2}T_{\mathds{P}(K_{\mathcal{C}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{C}})}\cong\mathcal{O}(2)
.
The section
(0,1)\in H^{0}(\mathcal{C},T_{\mathcal{C}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{C}})
induces
s\in H^{0}(\mathcal{O}(1))
, and
s^{\otimes 2}
defines the desired Poisson structure.
∎

Proposition 3.11
.

\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
has a natural Poisson structure, i.e. there exists a bilinear map

\begin{split}\widetilde{\theta}:\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}K_{\mathcal{X}})\otimes\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}K_{\mathcal{X}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}\end{split}

which defines a Poisson structure on
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
. Moreover, the restriction of
\widetilde{\theta}
to
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}^{L}
is also a Poisson structure.

Proof.

Let
\theta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{X},\wedge^{2}T\mathcal{X})
be the antisymmetric two tensor defined by the Poisson structure on
\mathcal{X}
. Consider the commutative diagram

(3.16)

\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\kern-125.25883pt\raise-46.27725pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{R\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1*}R\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(R\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}),\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}K_{\mathcal{X}})\otimes^{L}R\mathop{\rm R\Gamma(K_{\mathcal{X}})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

where the horizontal arrows come from cup product and trace map, and the last from the evaluation map. Passing to cohomology yields

(3.17)

\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 99.04182pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-99.04182pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise-11.43318pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\theta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 113.26819pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 113.26819pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}\otimes H^{2}(\mathcal{X},K^{2}_{\mathcal{X}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-34.29956pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cong}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-37.09273pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 113.26819pt\raise-22.86638pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 113.26819pt\raise-22.86638pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}\otimes H^{2}(\mathcal{X},K_{\mathcal{X}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\kern-95.95328pt\raise-45.73276pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}(R\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}),\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}K_{\mathcal{X}})\otimes\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 113.26819pt\raise-45.73276pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 113.26819pt\raise-45.73276pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}\otimes H^{2}(\mathcal{X},K_{\mathcal{X}})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

where the last arrow is a perfect pairing (see
[
Ni09
, Corollary 2.10]
). Thus the induced pairing

(3.18)

\begin{split}\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}K_{\mathcal{X}})\otimes\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}K_{\mathcal{X}})\\
\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}\otimes H^{2}(\mathcal{X},K^{2}_{\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{\theta}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}\otimes H^{2}(\mathcal{X},K_{\mathcal{X}})\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}.\end{split}

defines a Poisson structure
\widetilde{\theta}
on
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
. Equivalently, it corresponds to the map

\widetilde{B}:\ T^{\vee}\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}\;\cong\;\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}K_{\mathcal{X}})\;\xrightarrow{\ \theta\ }\;\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E})\;\cong\;T\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon},

see
[
Bo95
]
. Finally, the trace map yields a decomposition

\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}K_{\mathcal{X}})=\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}K_{\mathcal{X}})_{0}\oplus\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}\otimes H^{1}(\mathcal{X},K_{\mathcal{X}})\bigr),

orthogonal with respect to the pairing above. Since the trace-free part is identified with
T^{\vee}\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}^{L}
, the restriction of
\widetilde{\theta}
to
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}^{L}
is again Poisson.

∎

4
The Connectedness of the moduli spaces of sheaves

The aim of this section is to prove that
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
is connected. We first express the diagonal class of
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
in terms of Chern classes, then show that
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
is generated by the Künneth factors of the orbifold Chern character of a universal sheaf, and finally deduce the connectedness. Our main references are
[
Mk87
,
Bea92
,
Yo01
,
KLC06
,
Ma07
]
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Suppose that
K_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot H<0
or
K_{\mathcal{X}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
. Let
\mathbf{E}
be a (twisted) universal sheaf on
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}\times\mathcal{X}
.

(1)

The Poincaré dual of the diagonal
\delta
of
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}\times\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
is

\mathop{\rm c}\nolimits_{m}(-[R\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12*}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E}^{\vee}\otimes^{L}\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})])

where
m=\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
.

(2)

If
\mathbf{E}
is a universal sheaf, then the Künneth factors of the orbifold Chern character of
\mathbf{E}
generate the cohomology ring of
H^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon},\mathbb{C})
.

Proof of the case
K_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot H<0
.

Case I.

\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
is fine and
\mathbf{E}
is a universal sheaf. Consider the diagram

(4.1)

\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.68755pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 41.68755pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}\times\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}\times\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 100.27971pt\raise-10.47916pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 125.24205pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.09084pt\raise-10.47916pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.45699pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.31262pt\raise-15.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.31262pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-17.68755pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}\times\mathcal{X}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 49.32645pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}\times\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 122.9377pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}\times\mathcal{X}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

and the complex
R\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12*}R\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})
. Since
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}(E,F)=0
for any stable sheaves
E,F
, this complex is represented by a two-term complex
[W^{0}\xrightarrow{u}W^{1}]
, with
\mathop{\rm rk}\nolimits(W^{1})=\mathop{\rm rk}\nolimits(W^{0})+m-1
where
m=\dim\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
. For
z=(E_{1},E_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}\times\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
,
the exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(E_{2},E_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W^{0}\otimes k(z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u(z)}
\textstyle{W^{1}\otimes k(z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{1}(E_{2},E_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

shows that the
(r_{0}-1)
-st degeneracy locus
D_{r_{0}-1}
of
u
coincides with the diagonal
\delta
. By Thom–Porteous,
[\delta]=c_{m}(W^{1}-W^{0})\cap[\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}\times\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}]
, i.e. its Poincaré dual is

c_{m}\!\left(-[R\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12*}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E}^{\vee}\otimes^{L}\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})]\right)
. Using Proposition
C.10
, the K-theoretic pushforward satisfies

\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12!}([\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E}]^{\vee}\cdot[\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E}])=[R\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12*}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E}^{\vee}\otimes^{L}\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})].

By Töen-Riemann-Roch formula,

\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits([R\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12*}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E}^{\vee}\otimes^{L}\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})])=I\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12*}\left(\widetilde{\mathop{\rm ch}}\nolimits([\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E}]^{\vee})\cdot\widetilde{\mathop{\rm ch}}\nolimits([\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E}])\cdot\widetilde{\mathop{\rm td}}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{3}^{*}T\mathcal{X})\right)

By Künneth decomposition,
\widetilde{\mathop{\rm ch}}\nolimits(\mathbf{E})=\sum_{i}\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}^{*}\alpha_{i}\cup I\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}\beta_{i}
for some
\alpha_{i}\in H^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon},\mathbb{C})
and
\beta_{i}\in H^{*}(I\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C})
. Then

\mathop{\rm c}\nolimits_{m}(-[R\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12*}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E}^{\vee}\otimes^{L}\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})])=\sum_{i}\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}^{*}\alpha_{i}\cup\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}\gamma_{i}

with
\gamma_{i}\in H^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon},\mathbb{C})
. Thus
\{\alpha_{i}\}
generates
H^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon},\mathbb{C})
.

Case II.

\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
is not fine and
\mathbf{E}
a twisted universal sheaf. We will use some facts about twisted sheaves (see
[
Ca20
, Subsection 2.1]
for details). By the proof of Proposition
C.10
, we obtain a twisted locally free resolution
L^{\bullet}
of
\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E}
such that
R\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12*}R\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})=\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12*}\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(L^{\bullet},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})
, a complex of locally free sheaves satisfying the same universal property. Hence, as in Case I,
R\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12*}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E}^{\vee}\otimes^{L}\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})
is represented by a two-term complex, and the Poincaré dual of
\delta
is again
\mathop{\rm c}\nolimits_{m}(-[R\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12*}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E}^{\vee}\otimes^{L}\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})])
.

∎

Proof of the case:
K_{\mathcal{X}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

Using Lemma
4.2
, we obtain a complex of locally free sheaves

\textstyle{V_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{1}}

on
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}\times\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
with cohomologies
\mathop{\mathscr{H}}\nolimits_{-1}=0
,
\mathop{\mathscr{H}}\nolimits_{0}=\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})
,
\mathop{\mathscr{H}}\nolimits_{1}=\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{2}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})
, and
-r_{-1}+r_{0}-r_{1}=m-2
, where
r_{-1},r_{0},r_{1}
are the ranks of
V_{-1},V_{0},V_{1}
, respectively. Both
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{2}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})
and
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{2}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E})
are line bundles on the diagonal
\delta
. By
[
Ma02
, Lemma 4]
, the Poincaré dual of
\delta
is
c_{m}\!\left(-[R\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12*}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E}^{\vee}\otimes^{L}\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})]\right)
. Assuming
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
is fine with universal sheaf
\mathbf{E}
, we have

\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12!}([\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E}]^{\vee}\cdot[\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E}])=[R\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12*}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E}^{\vee}\otimes^{L}\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})].

Applying the Toen–Riemann–Roch formula shows that the Künneth factors of the orbifold Chern character of
\mathbf{E}
generate
H^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon},{\mathbb{C}})
.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

There exists a locally free sheaf
\mathbf{A}_{1}
and a short exact sequence of sheaves

(4.2)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

satisfying

(1)

\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{0}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits^{*}_{23}\mathbf{E},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits^{*}_{13}\mathbf{E})
=
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{2}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{0},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})
=
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits^{*}_{23}\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits^{*}_{13}\mathbf{E})
=
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{2}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits^{*}_{23}\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits^{*}_{13}\mathbf{E})=0
;

(2)

\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{0},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})\cong\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{2}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})
;

(3)

\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{0}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits^{*}_{23}\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits^{*}_{13}\mathbf{E})
is a locally free sheaf;

(4)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{0}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{0}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{0},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{1}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits^{*}_{13}\mathbf{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
.

Proof.

Fix a generating sheaf
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{X}
, and consider

\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{v}\times\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{v}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}.}

By the boundedness of semistable sheaves on Deligne-Mumford stacks (
[
Ni08
, Theorem 4.27 (2)]
), there is an integer
n
such that the Mumford-Castelnuovo regularity
{\rm reg}(E)\leq n
for any semistable sheaf
E
. The natural surjection
\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1}^{*}\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{1*}(\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\vee}(n))\otimes\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{2}^{*}\mathcal{E}(-n)\rightarrow\mathbf{E}
defines
\mathbf{A}_{1}
, giving exact sequence (
4.2
). Since
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{3}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E},\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{13}^{*}\mathbf{E})=0
(see Theorem
C.12
in Appendix
C
), the long exact sequence associated to (
4.2
) is

(4.3)

\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{0}_{\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{12}}(\mathop{\rm pr}\nolimits_{23}^{*}\mathbf{E},\mathop{\rm
