(see Proposition
C.6
in Appendix
C
).
Conditions (1)-(4) then follow directly.
∎

Corollary 4.3
.

\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
is connected.

Proof.

Using Theorem
4.1
and the base change theorem for relative Ext-sheaves on DM stacks (see Appendix
C
), the proof of this corollary can be completed by following the proof of Corollary 10 in
[
Ma07
]
.
∎

5
Orbifold Hilbert schemes

In this section we construct the Hilbert scheme
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
for
\alpha\in\mathcal{N}
(see Corollary
5.5
), prove its smoothness, and compute its dimension. Assuming
K_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot H<0
or
K_{\mathcal{X}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
, we show that
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
is connected (Theorem
5.7
). As a consequence, the Hilbert–Chow morphism
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{[\mathcal{O}_{q}]}(\mathcal{X})\to X
is the minimal resolution (Theorem
5.9
), and
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}([\mathbb{C}^{2}/G])
is connected for any finite small group
G
(Subsection
5.3
). We denote the set of stacky points of
\mathcal{X}
by
\{p_{k}\}_{k\in\mathfrak{I}}
, and write their images under
\pi
also as
p_{k}
.

5.1
Hilbert schemes

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a zero-dimensional closed substack of
\mathcal{X}
with coarse moduli space
Z
. Since
\mathcal{Z}
decomposes into a disjoint union of irreducible components, we will assume
\mathcal{Z}
is irreducible unless otherwise stated.

Lemma 5.1
.

For a stacky point
p
with stabilizer group
G
, there exists a cartesian diagram

(5.1)

\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 43.53082pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-43.53082pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!])/G]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-23.54387pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 58.5419pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 58.5419pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.2919pt\raise-16.20029pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\pi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.2919pt\raise-25.12282pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-35.4626pt\raise-32.40059pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]^{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.75719pt\raise-32.40059pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 57.75719pt\raise-32.40059pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

where the
G
-action on
\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]
is induced by the
G
-action on the cotangent space of
\mathcal{X}
at
p
. Moreover,
\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]^{G}
is isomorphic to the complete local ring
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p}
of
X
at
p
, and
\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]^{G}\rightarrow X
coincides with
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X,p})\rightarrow X
.

Proof.

By
[
Ol16
, Theorem 11.3.1]
, there exists an étale neighborhood
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(B)\to X
of
p
and a cartesian diagram

\textstyle{[\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A)/G]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X,}

with
B=A^{G}
. Let
\mathfrak{n}\subseteq B
be the maximal ideal corresponding to a lift
p^{\prime}\in\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(B)
of
p
, and let
\mathfrak{m}\subseteq A
be the unique maximal ideal over
\mathfrak{n}
. Then
\widehat{A}_{\mathfrak{m}}\cong\;A\otimes_{B}\widehat{B}_{\mathfrak{n}}
. By the Cohen structure theorem,
\widehat{A}_{\mathfrak{m}}\cong\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]
, hence
\widehat{B}_{\mathfrak{n}}=\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]^{G}
. This yields the cartesian diagram (
5.1
), where
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]^{G})\to X
identifies with the formal neighborhood of
p
.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

If the coarse moduli space
Z
of
\mathcal{Z}
satisfies
\mathop{\rm Supp}\nolimits(Z)=\{p\}
, then
\mathcal{Z}\;\cong\;[\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]/I_{\mathcal{Z}})/G]
for some
G
-invariant ideal
I_{\mathcal{Z}}
of finite colength.

Proof.

We may write
Z=\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(\mathcal{O}_{X,p}/I_{Z})
for some ideal
I_{Z}
of finite colength. Using Lemma
5.1
, we obtain a cartesian diagram

\textstyle{Z\times_{X}\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!])/G]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]^{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X.}

Since
\mathcal{Z}
is a closed substack of
Z\times_{X}\mathcal{X}
, it is also a closed substack of
[\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!])/G]
.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

If the decomposition of
A:=\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]/I_{\mathcal{Z}}
into irreducible representations is

\textstyle{A\cong\bigoplus_{0\leq i\leq t}\rho_{i}^{\oplus v_{i}}}\quad\text{for some integers $v_{i}$},

then
[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}]=\sum_{0\leq i\leq t}v_{i}[\mathcal{O}_{p}\otimes\rho_{i}]
in
K_{0}(\mathcal{X})
(see Section
A
for notation).
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

\big\{[\mathcal{O}_{q}],[\mathcal{O}_{p_{k}}\otimes\rho_{k,1}],[\mathcal{O}_{p_{k}}\otimes\rho_{k,2}],\ldots,[\mathcal{O}_{p_{k}}\otimes\rho_{k,r_{k}}]\big\}_{k\in\mathfrak{I}}
is linearly independent in
K^{\rm num}(\mathcal{X})
(see Definition
A.6
), where
q
is any non stacky point of
\mathcal{X}
and
\{\rho_{k,0},\cdots,\rho_{k,r_{k}}\}
are the sets of irreducible representations of
G_{k}
whose trivial representations are
\rho_{k,0}
.

Proof.

Recall the character of the regular representation of
G_{k}
is

\chi_{\rm reg}(g)=\begin{cases}|G_{k}|\quad\text{if $g$ is the identity element},\\
0\quad\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Using Proposition
A.3
and Corollary
A.8
, we obtain the lemma by a direct computation of the orbifold Chern characters.
∎

Corollary 5.5
.

\mathcal{N}:=\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{O}_{q}]+\sum_{k\in\mathfrak{I}}\sum_{1\leq i\leq r_{k}}\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{O}_{p_{k}}\otimes\rho_{k,i}]
is a lattice whose rank is
1+\sum_{k\in\mathfrak{I}}r_{k}
.
∎

Fix a K-class
\alpha=n_{0}[\mathcal{O}_{q}]+\sum_{k\in\mathfrak{I}}\sum_{1\leq i\leq r_{k}}n_{k,i}[\mathcal{O}_{p}\otimes\rho_{k,i}]\in\mathcal{N}
with
n_{0}\geq 0
and
n_{k,i}\geq 0
. Consider the Hilbert scheme

(5.2)

\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X}):=\{\text{closed substacks $\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathcal{X}$}|\quad[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}]=\alpha\}.

Proposition 5.6
.

\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
is a smooth projective scheme with dimension

(5.3)

\begin{split}2n_{0}+\sum_{k\in\mathfrak{I}}\bigg(\sum_{1\leq i\leq r_{k}}n_{k,i}\langle\chi_{\rho_{k,0}},\chi_{\rho_{k,i}\otimes\rho_{K_{\mathcal{X}}}}\rangle-\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq r_{k}}n_{k,i}n_{k,j}\big(\langle\chi_{\rho_{k,j}},\chi_{\rho_{k,i}}\rangle\\
+\langle\chi_{\rho_{k,j}},\chi_{\rho_{k,i}\otimes\rho_{K_{\mathcal{X}}}}\rangle-\langle\chi_{\rho_{k,j}},\chi_{\rho_{k,i}\otimes\rho_{\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}}}\rangle\big)\bigg).\end{split}

Proof.

First,
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}
,
\mathcal{Z}\mapsto I_{\mathcal{Z}}

is an isomorphism, where
I_{\mathcal{Z}}
is the ideal sheaf of
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}
is the moduli space of stable sheaves with trivial determinant and K-class
\upsilon=[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}]-\alpha\in K^{\rm num}(\mathcal{X})
. Hence
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
is a smooth projective scheme (Proposition
3.4
). The tangent space at
I_{\mathcal{Z}}
is
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{1}(I_{\mathcal{Z}},I_{\mathcal{Z}})_{0}
. Since
I_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\vee\vee}
is a line bundle, we have
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(I_{\mathcal{Z}},I_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes K_{\mathcal{X}})=\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}},K_{\mathcal{X}})
. By Serre duality,
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}(I_{\mathcal{Z}},I_{\mathcal{Z}})_{0}=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}},K_{\mathcal{X}})_{0}^{\vee}=0
. Using Euler characteristic (Appendix
A.2
),
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}})-\chi(I_{\mathcal{Z}},I_{\mathcal{Z}})
, where

\begin{split}\chi(I_{\mathcal{Z}},I_{\mathcal{Z}})&=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})-2n_{0}-\sum_{k\in\mathfrak{I}}\bigg(\sum_{1\leq i\leq r_{k}}n_{k,i}\langle\chi_{\rho_{k,0}},\chi_{\rho_{k,i}\otimes\rho_{K_{\mathcal{X}}}}\rangle\\
&+\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq r_{k}}n_{k,i}n_{k,j}(\langle\chi_{\rho_{k,j}},\chi_{\rho_{k,i}}\rangle+\langle\chi_{\rho_{k,j}},\chi_{\rho_{k,i}\otimes\rho_{K_{\mathcal{X}}}}\rangle-\langle\chi_{\rho_{k,j}},\chi_{\rho_{k,i}\otimes\rho_{\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}}}\rangle)\bigg)\end{split}

(see Proposition
A.5
).
∎

5.2
Hilbert-Chow morphism

Let
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}}\subset\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathcal{X}
be the universal closed substack with ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}_{\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}}
, flat over
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
. By the exactness of
(\pi\times\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits_{\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}})_{*}
(
[
AOV08
, Theorem 3.2]
),
(\pi\times\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits_{\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}})_{*}\mathcal{I}_{\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}}
is a sheaf of ideals on
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})\times X
, flat over
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
(
[
Ni08
, Corollary 1.3]
). Indeed,
(\pi\times\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits_{\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}})_{*}\mathcal{I}_{\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}}
is the ideal sheaf of the coarse moduli space of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}}
(see
[
Hu24
, the proof of Lemma 3.7]
), defining a morphism
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n_{0}}(X)
. Composing with the classical Hilbert-Chow morphism

(5.4)

\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n_{0}}(X)\rightarrow\mathop{\rm Sym}\nolimits^{n_{0}}(X),\quad Z\mapsto\sum_{z\in X(\mathbb{C})}\text{length}(\mathcal{O}_{Z,z})\cdot z,

we obtain the Hilbert-Chow morphism

h:\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathop{\rm Sym}\nolimits^{n_{0}}(X)
. For brevity, we denote
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n[\mathcal{O}_{q}]}(\mathcal{X})
by
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}(\mathcal{X})
.

Theorem 5.7
.

Assume that
K_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot H<0
or
K_{\mathcal{X}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

(1)

For any
\alpha\in\mathcal{N}
,
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
is a smooth connected projective scheme. Furthermore, if
\mathcal{X}
admits a Poisson structure, then
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
admits one as well.

(2)

The Hilbert-Chow morphism

(5.5)

h:\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}(\mathcal{X})\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Sym}\nolimits^{n}(X)

is a resolution of singularities. If
\mathcal{X}
equipped with a Poisson structure, then
(\ref{equ HC n})
is a Poisson resolution with respect to the induced Poisson structures.

Under our assumptions, Corollary
4.3
implies that
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
is a connected smooth projective scheme, where
\upsilon=[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}]-\alpha\in K^{\rm num}(\mathcal{X})
. If nonempty, there is an isomorphism

\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X}),\quad F\mapsto(\det(F),(\det(F)/F)\otimes\det(F)^{-1}).

By Proposition
2.7
,
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X})
is a complex torus and thus connected (via GAGA for DM stacks), so
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
is also connected.

If
\mathcal{X}
carries a Poisson structure, so does
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
(Proposition
3.11
). Moreover,
\mathop{\rm Sym}\nolimits^{n}(X)
is an irreducible
2n
-dimensional projective scheme, with
\mathop{\rm Sym}\nolimits^{n}(X^{*})
its open subset corresponding to the nonstacky locus
X^{*}
. Since
h
restricts to a Poisson resolution
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}(\mathcal{X}^{*})\to\mathop{\rm Sym}\nolimits^{n}(X^{*})
for
\mathcal{X}^{*}=\pi^{-1}(X^{*})
, it follows that
h
is a Poisson resolution for
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathop{\rm Sym}\nolimits^{n}(X)
as well
[
Fu05
]
.

Proof.

Under our assumption, Corollary
4.3
implies that
\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}
is a connected smooth projective scheme with
\upsilon=[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}]-\alpha\in K^{\rm num}(\mathcal{X})
. If nonempty, there is an isomorphism

\mathcal{M}_{\upsilon}\rightarrow\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X}),\quad F\mapsto(\mathop{\rm det}\nolimits(F),(\mathop{\rm det}\nolimits(F)/F)\otimes\mathop{\rm det}\nolimits(F)^{-1}).

By Proposition
2.7
,
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})
is a complex torus. Hence
\mathop{\rm Pic}\nolimits^{0}(\mathcal{X})
is connected (via GAGA theorem for DM stacks) and
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
is connected as well. If
\mathcal{X}
carries a Poisson structure, so does
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
(Proposition
3.11
). Moreover,
\mathop{\rm Sym}\nolimits^{n}(X)
is an irreducible projective scheme of dimension
2n
, with
\mathop{\rm Sym}\nolimits^{n}(X^{*})
its open subset corresponding to the nonstacky locus
X^{*}
. Since
h
restricts to a Poisson resolution
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}(\mathcal{X}^{*})\rightarrow\mathop{\rm Sym}\nolimits^{n}(X^{*})
for
\mathcal{X}^{*}=\pi^{-1}(X^{*})
, it follows that
h
is a Poisson resolution with respect to the induced Poisson structures (see
[
Fu05
]
).
∎

Remark 5.8
.

Alternatively, the tangent and cotangent spaces at an ideal sheaf
I_{\mathcal{Z}}
are
T_{I_{\mathcal{Z}}}\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})=\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(I_{\mathcal{Z}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}})
, and
T^{\vee}_{I_{\mathcal{Z}}}\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})=\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(I_{\mathcal{Z}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes K_{\mathcal{X}})
.
If
\theta\in H^{0}(\mathcal{X},\wedge^{2}T\mathcal{X})
is a Poisson structure on
\mathcal{X}
, it induces a morphism
B_{\theta}(I_{\mathcal{Z}}):\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(I_{\mathcal{Z}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes K_{\mathcal{X}})\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(I_{\mathcal{Z}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}})
, defining the Poisson structure on
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{\alpha}(\mathcal{X})
. Moreover, by
[
Bo98
, Lemma 3.2]
, the kernel of
B_{\theta}(I_{\mathcal{Z}})
is
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(I_{\mathcal{Z}},\mathop{\rm\mathcal{T}or}\nolimits_{1}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{D}}))
, where
\mathcal{D}
is the Cartier divisor determined by
\theta
.

Theorem 5.9
.

Under the hypothesis of Theorem
5.7
,
h:\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow X
is the minimal resolution.

Proof.

By
[
Ko07
, Proposition 2.18]
, it suffices to check étale-locally around the orbifold points of
X
. For an orbifold point, there exists an étale neighborhood
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A^{G})\to X
such that
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A^{G})\times_{X}\mathcal{X}\cong[\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A)/G]
, where
A
is a smooth connected affine surface with
G
-action. By
[
CT08
, Proposition 2.3]
,
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A^{G})\times_{X}\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{[\mathcal{O}_{q}]}(\mathcal{X})
is the component of
G
-
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A))
containing free
G
-orbits. Recall a
G
-cluster
Z
is a
G
-invariant finite subscheme of
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A)
with
H^{0}(\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A),\mathcal{O}_{Z})\cong\mathbb{C}[G]
. Since
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{|G|}(\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A))
is smooth
[
FGIKNV05
, Theorem 7.2.3 (2)]
and the
G
-action is linearizable,
G
-
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A))
is smooth. Denote
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A^{G})\times_{X}\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{[\mathcal{O}_{q}]}(\mathcal{X})
by
Y
.

Claim:

Y
is the minimal resolution of
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A^{G})
. Let
f:\widetilde{Y}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A^{G})
be the minimal resolution and
g:\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A)\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A^{G})
the quotient map. The graph
\Gamma_{g}:\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A)\hookrightarrow\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A)\times\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A^{G})
defines a
G
-equivariant surjection
\mathcal{O}_{\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A)\times\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A^{G})}\to\Gamma_{g*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A)}
. Pushing forward to
\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A^{G})
gives
\mathcal{O}_{\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A^{G})}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}A\to g_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A)}
, and pulling back along
f
yields
\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{Y}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}A\to f^{*}g_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A)}
. Modding out torsion,
f^{*}g_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A)}/{\rm torsion}
is locally free of rank
|G|

[
Es85
, Lemma 2.2]
, defining a
G
-cluster family over
\widetilde{Y}
. The induced morphism
\widetilde{Y}\to Y
is an isomorphism outside the singular fiber, so minimality of
\widetilde{Y}
implies
Y\cong\widetilde{Y}
.
∎

5.3
G
-equivariant Hilbert scheme on the affine plane

Let
G\subseteq\mathop{\textbf{\rm GL}}\nolimits_{2}(\mathbb{C})
be a small finite subgroup, that is a finite subgroup acting freely on
\mathbb{C}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
. Let
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}([\mathbb{C}^{2}/G])
be the scheme parametrising
G
-invariant ideals
I
in
\mathbb{C}[x,y]
such that
\mathbb{C}[x,y]/I
is isomorphic to the direct sum of n copies of regular representation of
G
.

Proposition 5.10
.

\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}([\mathbb{C}^{2}/G])
is connected.

Proof.

Define a
G
-representation
\mathbb{C}^{3}
by

G\rightarrow\mathop{\textbf{\rm GL}}\nolimits_{3}(\mathbb{C})\quad g\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}g&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

and then induce a
G
-action on
\mathds{P}^{2}
. The quotient
[\mathds{P}^{2}/G]
compactifies
[\mathbb{C}^{2}/G]
. The coarse space map factors as

(5.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathds{P}^{2}/G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathds{P}^{2}/G)}^{\rm can}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{P}^{2}/G}

where
(\mathds{P}^{2}/G)^{\rm can}
is the canonical stack. The top arrow is an isomorphism over
[\mathbb{C}^{2}/G]
(
[
GS17
, Theorem 1]
). Hence
(\mathds{P}^{2}/G)^{\rm can}
is also a compactification of
[\mathbb{C}^{2}/G]
, and then
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}([\mathbb{C}^{2}/G])
is an open subscheme of
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}((\mathds{P}^{2}/G)^{\rm can})
. For some sufficient large integer
N
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathds{P}^{2}}(N)
descends to a very ample line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathds{P}^{2}/G}(H)
on
\mathds{P}^{2}/G
. Note that
K_{(\mathds{P}^{2}/G)^{\rm can}}\cdot H<0
. By Corollary
4.3
,
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}([\mathbb{C}^{2}/G])
is a connected smooth quasiprojective scheme of dimension
2n
.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

When
G
is abelian, Bejleri and Zaimi have proved that
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}([\mathbb{C}^{2}/G])
is connected (see
[
BZ
, Corollary 2.3]
). For
G\subset\mathop{\textbf{\rm SL}}\nolimits_{2}
,
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{n}([\mathbb{C}^{2}/G])
is connected (see Ibid., footnote on page 2).

6
Compactification of the Hitchin systems

In this section we restrict to the two-dimensional Hitchin systems corresponding to the affine Dynkin diagrams
\widetilde{A}_{0}
,
\widetilde{D}_{4}
,
\widetilde{E}_{6}
,
\widetilde{E}_{7}
and
\widetilde{E}_{8}
, constructed by Groechenig (
[
Go14
]
). Concretely, they are moduli spaces of orbifold Higgs bundles on
E
,
\mathds{P}_{2,2,2,2}^{1}
,
\mathds{P}_{3,3,3}^{1}
,
\mathds{P}_{4,4,2}^{1}
,
\mathds{P}_{6,3,2}^{1}
, where
E
is an elliptic curve and
\mathds{P}^{1}_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{s}}
denotes an orbifold curve with coarse moduli space
\mathds{P}^{1}
and
s
orbifold points of the specified orders. These are exactly all the one-dimensional Calabi-Yau orbifolds. Except
E
, each arises as a nontrivial quotient of an elliptic curve by a cyclic group:

[E_{2}/\mu_{2}],\quad[E_{3}/\mu_{3}],\quad[E_{4}/\mu_{4}],\quad[E_{6}/\mu_{6}].

For brevity, we denote the
\mu_{i}
-Hilbert scheme
\mu_{i}
-
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits(T^{\vee}E_{i})
by
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i})
.

Theorem 6.1

(
[
Go14
]
)
.

\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i})
is isomorphic to a two-dimensional moduli space
\mathcal{M}(i)
of stable orbifold Higgs bundles on
\mathcal{X}_{i}:=[E_{i}/\mu_{i}]
. In particular these moduli spaces are crepant resolutions of the GIT quotients
T^{\vee}E_{i}/\mu_{i}
.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

Indeed, Groechenig proved that the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
\mu_{i}
-
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits(T^{\vee}E_{i})
induced by the natural
\mathbb{C}^{*}
action on
T^{\vee}E_{i}
coincides with the natural
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
\mathcal{M}(i)
, although this is not stated explicitly in his paper. In addition, this isomorphism is a symplectomorphism with respect to their natural symplectic structures (see
[
Jia25
]
).

We have

T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}=[E_{i}\times\mathbb{C}/\mu_{i}],\qquad\hbox to189.36pt{\vbox to51.66pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 94.67642pt\lower-25.82838pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

and since the unit element of
E_{i}
has stabilizer
\mu_{i}
, it follows that
\psi_{i}
admits a section.

Proposition 6.3
.

Composing the Hilbert-Chow morphism with the coarse map of
\psi_{i}
yields

(6.1)

\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i})\rightarrow E_{i}\times\mathbb{C}/\mu_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}/\mu_{i}\cong\mathbb{C}

which is isomorphic the corresponding Hitchin map.

Proof.

Consider the cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i})\times T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}\times_{\mathcal{Y}_{i}}T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i})\times T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i})\times T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i})\times\mathcal{Y}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\times\psi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\times\psi_{i}}

By
[
Go14
, Lemma 3.6, Proposition 4.2]
and the fact that the Abel-Jacobi map is
\mu_{i}
-equivariant, we obtain an equivalence of derived categories of Fourier-Mukai type relative to
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i})\times\mathcal{Y}_{i}

D_{\rm coh}^{b}(\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i})\times T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i})\cong D_{\rm coh}^{b}(\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i})\times T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}).

Using BNR correspondence,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}}}
corresponds the universal family of Higgs bundles on
\mathcal{X}_{i}
under above equivalence. The Fitting-support of
(id\times\psi_{i})_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}}}
defines a
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}
-flat family of effective cartier divisors of degree one, whose base is the universal spectral curves of the corresponding Higgs bundles.
We then get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where the left vertical arrow is (
6.1
) and the right one is the Hitchin map. Since
\psi_{i}
has a section, below arrow is an isomorphism as well.

∎

Consider the formal power series ring
\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]
with the natural
\mu_{r}
-action
\zeta_{r}(x)=\zeta_{r}\cdot x
and
\zeta_{r}(y)=\zeta_{r}\cdot y
, where
\zeta_{r}
is a primitive
r
-th root of unit. The Hilbert scheme
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{r}(\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!])
parametrizes ideals of colength
r
. Its
\mu_{r}
-fixed locus decomposes as

\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{r}(\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!])^{\mu_{r}}=\coprod_{\rho\in K(\mu_{r})}\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{r}_{\rho}(\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!])

where
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{r}_{\rho}(\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!])=\{I\in\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{r}(\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!])^{\mu_{r}}|\quad\text{$\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]/I\cong\rho$ as $\mu_{r}$-representations}\}
. For the regular representation
\rho_{\rm reg}
,

\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{r}_{\rho_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!])
is usually written as
\mu_{r}
-
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits(\mathds{C}[\![x,y]\!])
.

Lemma 6.4
.

\mu_{r}
-
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits(\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!])\cong\mathds{P}^{1}
.

Proof.

Since any
I\in\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{r}_{\rho_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!])
can be represented as
I=(ax+by)+(x,y)^{r}

for some
[a,b]\in\mathds{P}^{1}
, we complete the proof.
∎

Theorem 6.5
.

The two-dimensional Hitchin systems for
\widetilde{D}_{4}
,
\widetilde{E}_{6}
,
\widetilde{E}_{7}
, and
\widetilde{E}_{8}
admit natural compactifications

\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}\!\bigl(\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{i}})\bigr)

with the following properties:

(1)

The natural
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action and Poisson structure on
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{i}}))
are compatible with, and extend, the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action and symplectic structure on
\mathcal{M}(i)
.

(2)

The Hitchin maps extend to the compositions

\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}\!\bigl(\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{i}})\bigr)\xrightarrow{\,h_{i}\,}E\times\mathds{P}^{1}/\mu_{i}\longrightarrow\mathds{P}^{1}/\mu_{i}\cong\mathds{P}^{1},

where
h_{i}
are the Hilbert–Chow morphisms.

(3)

Each
h_{i}
is the minimal resolution of the GIT quotient
\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}E_{i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{E_{i}})/\mu_{i}
, and provides a Poisson resolution.

(4)

The boundary (with reduced structure) consists of
s+1
copies of
\mathds{P}^{1}
, where
s
is the number of orbifold points of
\mathcal{X}_{i}
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i})
is an open subscheme of
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{i}}))
. Since
\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{i}})
carries a Poisson structure
\theta
extending the symplectic form on
T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}
(Proposition
3.10
),
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{i}}))
is a connected smooth Poisson projective surface, and the Hilbert-Chow morphism is a minimal (Poisson) resolution (Theorem
5.7
, Theorem
5.9
and
[
Fu05
, Proposition 3.3]
). By Remark
6.2
, the natural
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action and Poisson structure on
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{i}}))
are compatible with, and extend, the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action and symplectic structure on
\mathcal{M}(i)
. By Proposition
6.3
, the condition (2) is immediate. The degenerate locus of
\theta
is the divisor
\mathcal{D}=2\cdot\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i})
, so the degenerate locus of the natural Poisson structure
B_{\theta}
on
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{i}}))
is

\{\mathcal{Z}\in\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{i}}))|\quad\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{D}\neq\emptyset\}

(Remark
5.8
), which is the complement of
\mu_{r}
-
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits(T^{\vee}E)
in

\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{i}}))
. With reduced structure, it is

\textstyle{\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}))\bigcup_{i=1}^{s}\text{$\mu_{a_{i}}$-$\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits(\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!])$}}.

Since the corase moduli space of
\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i})
is
\mathds{P}^{1}
, then
\mathop{\rm Hilb}\nolimits^{1}(\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{i}))
is isomorphic to
\mathds{P}^{1}
. We complete the proof by applying Lemma
6.4
.

∎

In what follows, we will discuss each case individually.

6.1
\widetilde{D}_{4}
-case

Recall that any elliptic curve can be written in Weierstrass form
E_{(a,b)}:y^{2}=x^{3}+ax+b
. The involution given by negation in the group law is
\tau:E_{(a,b)}\rightarrow E_{(a,b)}
,
(x,y)\mapsto(x,-y)
, and induces an
\mu_{2}
-action on
E_{(a,b)}
with four fixed points. Consider the orbifold curve
\mathcal{X}_{2}=[E_{(a,b)}/\mu_{2}]
whose coarse moduli space is
\mathds{P}^{1}
with four orbifold points
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
,
p_{3}
,
p_{4}
. The differential
\omega=dx/2y=dy/(3x^{2}+a)
is a globally defined one-form on
E_{(a,b)}
satisfying
\tau^{*}\omega=-\omega
. Hence
\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{2}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{2}})=[E_{(a,b)}\times\mathds{P}^{1}/\mu_{2}]
, where the
\mu_{2}
-action on
E_{(a,b)}\times\mathds{P}^{1}
is
\tau:(p,[z_{0},z_{1}])\mapsto(\tau(p),[z_{0},-z_{1}])
.
The projections
E\times\mathds{P}^{1}\rightarrow E_{(a,b)}
and
E_{(a,b)}\times\mathds{P}^{1}\rightarrow\mathds{P}^{1}
are
\mu_{2}
-equivariant, hence they descent to two morphisms

(6.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathds{P}^{1}\cong E_{(a,b)}/\mu_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathds{P}^{1}/\mu_{2}\cong\mathds{P}^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathop{\rm pr}}\nolimits_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathop{\rm pr}}\nolimits_{2}}

where
X_{2}=E_{(a,b)}\times\mathds{P}^{1}/\mu_{2}
. Composing these with the minimal resolution
\widetilde{\pi}_{2}:\widetilde{X}_{2}\rightarrow X_{2}
yields two fibrations

(6.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{P}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{P}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

Lemma 6.6
.

\pi_{2}^{\prime}:\widetilde{X}_{2}\rightarrow\mathds{P}^{1}
is a generically
\mathds{P}^{1}
-fibration, which has exactly four singular fibers over the four orbifold points. More precisely, there exist smooth rational curves
\{D_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 4}
,
\{E_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 4}
,
\{F_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 4}
such that
\pi_{2}^{\prime-1}(p_{i})=2D_{i}+E_{i}+F_{i}
, whose dual graphs are
-2
-1
-2
E_{i}
D_{i}
F_{i}

Proof.

Suppose that
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
,
q_{3}
,
q_{4}
are the fixed points of
\tau
, corresponding respectively to
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
,
p_{3}
,
p_{4}
. For each
q_{i}
, we obtain a smooth rational curve
\widetilde{D}_{i}=\{q_{i}\}\times\mathds{P}^{1}/\mu_{2}
on
X_{2}
, and

(6.4)

\widetilde{\mathop{\rm pr}}\nolimits_{1}^{-1}(p_{i})=2\widetilde{D}_{i}.

The only singular points of
X_{2}
lying on
\widetilde{D}_{i}
are
0
and
\infty
. Let
E_{i}
and
F_{i}
be the exceptional curves over the singular points
0
,
\infty
, respectively. Since all the singular points of
X_{2}
are of type
\frac{1}{2}(1,1)
, we have
E_{i}^{2}=F_{i}^{2}=-2
and
K_{\widetilde{X}_{2}}\cdot E_{i}=K_{\widetilde{X}_{2}}\cdot F_{i}=0
. From (
6.4
), it follows that
\pi_{2}^{\prime-1}(p_{i})=2D_{i}+n_{i}E_{i}+m_{i}F_{i}
for some natural numbers
n_{i}
and
m_{i}
, where
D_{i}
denotes the strict transform of
\widetilde{D}_{i}
on
\widetilde{X}_{2}
. Intersecting with
E_{i}
gives
(2D_{i}+n_{i}E_{i}+m_{i}F_{i})\cdot E_{i}=0
, and hence
n_{i}=D_{i}\cdot E_{i}
. Analogously, we have
m_{i}=D_{i}\cdot F_{i}
. Hence, we obtain
2D_{i}^{2}+n_{i}^{2}+m_{i}^{2}=0
, so in particular
D_{i}^{2}<0
. By the adjunction formula,
(2D_{i}+n_{i}E_{i}+m_{i}F_{i})\cdot K_{\widetilde{X}_{2}}=-2
, which implies
D_{i}\cdot K_{\widetilde{X}_{2}}=-1
. Thus
D_{i}
is an exceptional curve of first kind, and consequently,
n_{i}=m_{i}=1
.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

The elliptic fibration
\pi_{2}:\widetilde{X}_{2}\rightarrow\mathds{P}^{1}
is relatively minimal and the singular fibers are the fibers over
0
and
\infty
, which are of type
I_{0}^{\ast}(\widetilde{D}_{4})
.

Proof.

Note that
\widetilde{\mathop{\rm pr}}\nolimits_{2}^{-1}(0)=2\widetilde{D}_{0}
, where
\widetilde{D}_{0}=E_{(a,b)}\times\{0\}/\mu_{2}
is a smooth rational curve on
X_{2}
containing four singular points of type
\frac{1}{2}(1,1)
. Hence
\pi_{2}^{-1}(0)=2D_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\widetilde{n}_{i}E_{i}
with
D_{0}
the strict transformation of
\widetilde{D}_{0}
in
\widetilde{X}_{2}
and
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
. For each
E_{i}
,
(2D_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{4}\widetilde{n}_{j}E_{j})\cdot E_{i}=0
gives
\widetilde{n}_{i}=D_{0}\cdot E_{i}
. Consequently,
2D_{0}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\widetilde{n}_{i}^{2}=0
and
D_{0}^{2}\leq-2
. By the adjunction formula,
K_{\widetilde{X}_{2}}\cdot(2D_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{4}\widetilde{n}_{j}E_{j})=0
, and then
K_{\widetilde{X}_{2}}\cdot D_{0}=0
. Since
\frac{1}{2}(D_{0}\cdot D_{0}+K_{\widetilde{X}_{2}}\cdot D_{0})+1
is a nonnegative integer, we obtain
D_{0}^{2}=-2
and
\widetilde{n}_{1}=\widetilde{n}_{2}=\widetilde{n}_{3}=\widetilde{n}_{4}=-1
. Analogously, we can show that
D_{\infty}
satisfies similar equations.
∎

For the second Hirzebruch surface
h_{2}:H_{2}=\mathds{P}(\mathcal{O}(2)\oplus\mathcal{O})\rightarrow\mathds{P}^{1}
, the zero-section
E_{0}
of
H_{2}
is the image of the section
(0,1)
of
\mathcal{O}(2)\oplus\mathcal{O}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{O}(2)\subset\mathcal{O}(2)\oplus\mathcal{O}
determines a smooth rational curve
\mathds{P}(\mathcal{O}(2))
on
H_{2}
, which we denoted by
E_{\infty}
. Let
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
,
C_{3}
,
C_{4}
be the fibers of
h_{2}
over the points
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
,
p_{3}
,
p_{4}
, respectively. First, we blow up successively the points
E_{0}\cap C_{1}
,
E_{0}\cap C_{2}
,
E_{0}\cap C_{3}
,
E_{0}\cap C_{4}
, which yields a new surface and a morphism
h_{2}^{(1)}:H_{2}^{(1)}\rightarrow\mathds{P}^{1}
. The resulting exceptional divisors are denoted by
E_{1}^{(1)}
,
E_{2}^{(1)}
,
E_{3}^{(1)}
,
E_{4}^{(1)}
. Next, let
\widetilde{C}_{1}
,
\widetilde{C}_{2}
,
\widetilde{C}_{3}
,
\widetilde{C}_{4}
denote the strict transforms of
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
,
C_{3}
,
C_{4}
. Blowing up the four points
\widetilde{C}_{1}\cap E_{1}^{(1)}
,
\widetilde{C}_{2}\cap E_{2}^{(1)}
,
\widetilde{C}_{3}\cap E_{3}^{(1)}
,
\widetilde{C}_{4}\cap E_{4}^{(1)}
, we obtain a new surface
h_{2}^{(2)}:H_{2}^{(2)}\rightarrow\mathds{P}^{1}
.

.

\pi_{2}^{\prime}:\widetilde{X}_{2}\rightarrow\mathds{P}^{1}

.

and

).

6.2
\widetilde{E}_{6}
-case

For the elliptic curve
E_{(0,1)}:y^{2}=x^{3}+1
, the automorphism
\sigma_{3}:(x,y)\mapsto(e^{2\pi\sqrt{-1}/3}x,y)
defines a
\mu_{2}
-action. For the orbifold curve
\mathcal{X}_{3}=[E_{(0,1)}/\mu_{3}]
, since
\sigma_{3}^{*}\omega=e^{2\pi\sqrt{-1}/3}\omega
, we have
\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{3}\oplus\mathcal{O})=[E_{(0,1)}\times\mathds{P}^{1}/\mu_{3}]
, where the
\mu_{3}
-action is
(p,[z_{0},z_{1}])\mapsto(\sigma_{3}(p),[z_{0},e^{4\pi\sqrt{-1}/3}z_{1}])
. As in the
\widetilde{D}_{4}
-case, we have two diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{P}^{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathop{\rm pr}}\nolimits_{2}}

Lemma 6.9
.

\pi_{3}^{\prime}:\widetilde{X}_{3}\rightarrow\mathds{P}^{1}
is a generically
\mathds{P}^{1}
-fibration, whose singular fibers are the ones over the orbifold points
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
,
p_{3}
of
\mathcal{X}_{3}
. More precisely, there exist smooth rational curves
\{D_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 3}
,
\{E_{ij}\}_{1\leq i\leq 2,1\leq j\leq 3}
,
\{F_{k}\}_{1\leq k\leq 3}
such that
\pi_{3}^{\prime-1}(p_{l})=3D_{l}+E_{1l}+2E_{2l}+F_{l}
, whose dual graphs are
-2
-2
-1
-3
E_{1l}
E_{2l}
D_{l}
F_{l}

Proof.

Following the proof of Lemma
6.6
, we can show that there exists a smooth rational curve
\widetilde{D}_{i}
on
X_{3}
such that
\widetilde{\mathop{\rm pr}}\nolimits_{1}^{-1}(p_{i})=3\widetilde{D}_{i}
. The singularities of
X_{3}
on
\widetilde{D}_{i}
are of type
\frac{1}{3}(1,2)
at
0
and of type
\frac{1}{3}(1,1)
at
\infty
. The corresponding exceptional curves are
E_{1i}\cup E_{2i}
and
F_{i}
, where
E_{1i}
,
E_{2i}
and
F_{i}
are smooth rational curves satisfying
E_{1i}^{2}=E_{2i}^{2}=-2
,
E_{1i}\cdot E_{2i}=1
and
F_{i}^{2}=-3
(see
[
Rie77
, Satz 8]
). Thus
\pi_{3}^{-1}(p_{i})=3D_{i}+n_{1i}E_{1i}+n_{2i}E_{2i}+m_{i}F_{i}
with integers
n_{1i}
,
n_{2i}
,
m_{i}
. Intersecting with
E_{1i}
,
E_{2i}
,
F_{i}
yields
3D_{i}\cdot E_{1i}-2n_{1i}+n_{2i}=0
,
3D_{i}\cdot E_{2i}-2n_{2i}+n_{1i}=0
and
3D_{i}\cdot F_{i}-3m_{i}=0
. Hence,
9D_{i}^{2}+n_{1i}(2n_{1i}-n_{2i})+n_{2i}(2n_{2i}-n_{1i})+3m_{i}^{2}=0
, so
D_{i}^{2}<0
. By the adjunction formula,
K_{\widetilde{X}_{3}}\cdot(3D_{i}+n_{1i}E_{1i}+n_{2i}E_{2i}+m_{i}F_{i})=-2
, which implies
3K_{\widetilde{X}_{3}}\cdot D_{i}=-2-m_{i}
. Thus,
D_{i}
is an exceptional curve of the first kind, with
D_{i}\cdot D_{i}=K_{\widetilde{X}_{3}}\cdot D_{i}=-1
and
m_{i}=1
. Finally,
n_{1i}^{2}+n_{2i}^{2}-n_{1i}n_{2i}=3
, so
(n_{1i},n_{2i})
is
(1,2)
or
(2,1)
. Up to order, we take
n_{1i}=1
and
n_{2i}=2
.
∎

Lemma 6.10
.

The elliptic fibration
\pi_{3}:\widetilde{X}_{3}\rightarrow\mathds{P}^{1}
has only two singular fibers, which are the ones over
0
and
\infty
. Specifically, there exist smooth rational curves

,

(1)

(\widetilde{E}_{6})

Proof.

As before, there exists a smooth rational curve

containing three singular points of type
\frac{1}{3}(1,2)
with
\widetilde{\mathop{\rm pr}}\nolimits_{2}^{-1}(0)=3\widetilde{D}_{0}
. Thus
\pi_{3}^{-1}(0)=3D_{0}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2,\,1\leq j\leq 3}\widetilde{n}_{ij}E_{ij}
, where
D_{0}
is the strict transform of
\widetilde{D}_{0}
, and
\{\widetilde{n}_{ij}\}_{1\leq i\leq 2,\,1\leq j\leq 3}

, and so

. By the adjunction formula,

,

and

. Following Lemma 2.12 (ii) in

]
, the intersection matrix of

with

corresponds to the affine Dynkin diagram

. The projection formula gives

, hence

, so

and

. This completes the proof of case (1) by Zariski’s Lemma (see ibid). Analogously, let

. Then

with
\widetilde{m}_{i}=D_{\infty}\cdot F_{i}
, which gives
3D_{\infty}^{2}+\widetilde{m}_{1}^{2}+\widetilde{m}_{2}^{2}+\widetilde{m}_{3}^{2}=0
and
K_{\widetilde{X}_{3}}\cdot D_{\infty}<0
. Hence,
D_{\infty}
is an exceptional curve of the first kind with

\widetilde{m}_{1}=\widetilde{m}_{2}=\widetilde{m}_{3}=1
, as illustrated in Figure

.

Let

6.3
\widetilde{E}_{7}
-case

The elliptic curve
E_{(1,0)}:y^{2}=x^{3}+x
admits a
\mu_{4}
-action defined by
\sigma_{4}:E_{(1,0)}\to E_{(1,0)}
,
(x,y)\mapsto(-x,\sqrt{-1}\,y)
. The quotient stack
\mathcal{X}_{4}=[E_{(1,0)}/\mu_{4}]
has three orbifold points
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
, and
p_{3}
with stabilizer groups
\mu_{4}
,
\mu_{4}
, and
\mu_{2}
, respectively.
Since
\sigma_{4}^{*}\omega=\sqrt{-1}\,\omega
, we obtain
\mathds{P}(T^{\vee}\mathcal{X}_{4}\oplus\mathcal{O})=[E_{(1,0)}\times\mathds{P}^{1}/\mu_{4}]
, where the
\mu_{4}
-action is given by

\sigma_{4}:E_{(1,0)}\times\mathds{P}^{1}\to E_{(1,0)}\times\mathds{P}^{1},\quad(p,[z_{0},z_{1}])\mapsto(\sigma_{4}(p),[z_{0},-\sqrt{-1}\,z_{1}]).

In addition, the orbifold surface
[E_{(0,1)}\times\mathds{P}^{1}/\mu_{4}]
has six orbifold points; equivalently, the coarse space
X_{4}=(E_{(0,1)}\times\mathds{P}^{1})/\mu_{4}
has exactly six singular points. Analogously, we obtain the following two diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}_{4}}

Lemma 6.12
.

\pi_{4}^{\prime}:\widetilde{X}_{4}\rightarrow\mathds{P}^{1}
is a generically
\mathds{P}^{1}
-bundle whose singular fibers are exactly the ones over the orbifold points of
\mathcal{X}_{4}
. More precisely, there exist smooth rational curves
\{D_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 3}
,
\{E_{ij}\}_{1\leq i\leq 3,1\leq j\leq 2}
,
\{F_{k}\}_{1\leq k\leq 2}
,
\{E,F\}
such that

(1)

for
l=1,2
, we have
\pi_{4}^{\prime-1}(p_{l})=4D_{l}+E_{1l}+2E_{2l}+3E_{3l}+F_{l}
, whose dual graphs are
-2
-2
-2
-1
-4
E_{1l}
E_{2l}
E_{3l}
D_{l}
F_{l}

(2)

\pi_{4}^{\prime-1}(p_{3})=2D_{3}+E+F
with dual graph
-2
-1
-2
E
D_{3}
F

Proof.

It suffices to prove case (1) when
l=1
. As before, there exists a smooth rational curve
\widetilde{D}_{1}
on
X_{4}
such that
\widetilde{\mathop{\rm pr}}\nolimits_{1}^{-1}(p_{1})=4\widetilde{D}_{1}
, on which lie two singular points of
X_{4}
of types
\tfrac{1}{4}(1,3)
and
\tfrac{1}{4}(1,1)
. The corresponding exceptional curves are denoted by
E_{11}\cup E_{21}\cup E_{31}
and
F_{1}
, respectively, and they satisfy
E_{11}^{2}=E_{21}^{2}=E_{31}^{2}=-2
,
E_{11}\cdot E_{21}=E_{21}\cdot E_{31}=1
,
E_{11}\cdot E_{31}=0
and
F_{1}^{2}=-4
. Let
D_{1}
be the strict transform of
\widetilde{D}_{1}
on
\widetilde{X}_{4}
. We may write
\pi_{4}^{\prime-1}(p_{1})=4D_{1}+n_{1}E_{11}+n_{2}E_{21}+n_{3}E_{31}+mF_{1}
for some natural numbers
n_{1},n_{2},n_{3},m
. Intersecting with itself yields
16D_{1}^{2}+(2n_{1}^{2}+2n_{2}^{2}+2n_{3}^{2}-2n_{1}n_{2}-2n_{2}n_{3})+4m^{2}=0
, so
D_{1}^{2}<0
. By the adjunction formula, we obtain

(4D_{1}+n_{1}E_{11}+n_{2}E_{21}+n_{3}E_{31}+mF_{1})\cdot K_{\widetilde{X}_{4}}=-2
, which implies
2D_{1}\cdot K_{\widetilde{X}_{4}}=-1-m
. Hence
D_{1}
is an exceptional curve and necessarily
m=1
. Up to reordering, we find
n_{1}=1
,
n_{2}=2
and
n_{3}=3
.

∎

.

, the surface

. Beginning with

, We may carry out a sequence of blow-downs, and then obtain a

-fibration which is a Hirzebruch surface

for some
n
. Since
D_{0}
remains unchanged throughout this process, it follows from

that the resulting Hirzebruch surface is the second one. Reversing this process, we can recover the original surface
\widetilde{X}_{4}
. Then
\widetilde{X}_{4}
is precisely
H_{2}^{(4)}
, as deduced from the construction of
H_{2}^{(4)}
. The proof is complete.

,

, which gives

,
D_{1}\cdot E_{5}=1
and
D_{1}\cdot F_{1}=1
.

∎

7
Appendix

Throughout this appendix, we always assume that
\mathcal{X}
is a Deligne-Mumford stack of finite type over
k
with finite diagonal, whose coarse moduli space is
\pi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow X
.

Appendix A
Orbifold Chern character and Euler form

Without loss of generality, assume
\mathcal{X}
is a projective orbifold surface with a single stacky point
p
, whose stabilizer group is
G
. Let
\iota:BG\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}
denote the residue gerbe at
p
. Define
\mathcal{O}_{p}:=\iota_{*}\mathcal{O}_{BG}
and, for any
G
-representation
\rho
, set
\mathcal{O}_{p}\otimes\rho:=\iota_{*}V_{\rho}
, where
V_{\rho}
is the locally free sheaf on
BG
corresponding to
\rho
. For
g\in G
, let
(g)
be its conjugacy class,
C(g)
its centralizer, and
\mathcal{T}
the set of conjugacy classes of
G
. Since
K_{0}(BG)\cong K(G)
, the pullback
\iota^{*}
induces a homomorphism
\iota^{*}:K_{0}(\mathcal{X})\to K(G)
, and for any coherent sheaf
E
on
\mathcal{X}
, we write
\rho_{E}:=\iota^{*}[E]
for the associated virtual
G
-representation.

A.1
Orbifold Chern character

The inertia stack decomposes as

I\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}\,\coprod\,\coprod_{(g)\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{(1)\}}BC(g),

and its cohomology splits accordingly:

(A.1)

H^{*}(I\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C})=H^{*}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C})\,\oplus\,\bigoplus_{(g)\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{(1)\}}H^{*}(BC(g),\mathbb{C}).

With respect to this decomposition, the orbifold Chern character of a coherent sheaf
E
is

\widetilde{\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits}(E)=\big(\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits(E),(\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits^{(g)}(E))_{(g)\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{(1)\}}\big),

where
\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits(E)\in H^{\rm even}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C})
and
\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits^{(g)}(E)\in\mathbb{C}
.

Lemma A.1
.

For
(g)\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{(1)\}
,
\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits^{(g)}(E)=\chi_{\rho_{E}}(g)
.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Let
G
act on
\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]
via a 2-dimensional representation
\tau
. Then there is a
G
-equivariant exact sequence

(A.2)

0\to\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]\otimes\det(\tau)\to\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]\otimes\tau\to\mathbb{C}[\![x,y]\!]\to\mathbb{C}\to 0.

∎

Proposition A.3
.

Let
\rho
be a
G
-representation of degree
d
. Then

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits(\mathcal{O}_{p}\otimes\rho)=(0,0,d/|G|),

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits^{(g)}(\mathcal{O}_{p}\otimes\rho)=\mathop{\rm det}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits-\rho_{\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}}(g))\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\rho(g)),\quad(g)\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{(1)\}.

Proof.

By applying Lemma 1, the conclusion of this proposition follows by direct computation
∎

A.2
Euler form and
K^{\rm num}(\mathcal{X})

As in the case of schemes, the Euler characteristic of a pair
(E_{1},E_{2})
of coherent sheaves is

\chi(E_{1},E_{2})=\sum_{0\leq i\leq 2}(-1)^{i}\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{\mathbb{C}}\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{i}(E_{1},E_{2}).

By Toën-Riemann-Roch formula, we have

\chi(E_{1},E_{2})=\int_{I\mathcal{X}}\widetilde{\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits}(E_{1}^{\vee})\widetilde{\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits}(E_{2})\widetilde{\mathop{\rm td}\nolimits}(T\mathcal{X}).

Here
(\cdot)^{\vee}:K_{0}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow K_{0}(\mathcal{X})
is the involution, which extends the operation “taking the dual of a locally free sheaf” linearly to whole
K_{0}(\mathcal{X})
.

Definition A.4
.

The Euler form
\bar{\chi}:K_{0}(\mathcal{X})\times K_{0}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathds{Z}
is a biadditive integer-valued function determined by
\bar{\chi}([E_{1}],[E_{2}]):=\chi(E_{1},E_{2})
.

By a straightforward computation, it is easy to prove the following formulas.

Proposition A.5
.

(1)

\chi(E_{1},E_{2})=\chi(E_{2},E_{1}\otimes K_{\mathcal{X}})
.

(2)

\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})
, where
\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})
is the Euler characteristic of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
;

(3)

\chi(\mathcal{O}_{q},\mathcal{O}_{q})=0
and
\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{O}_{q})=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{q},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})=1
;

(4)

\chi(\mathcal{O}_{p}\otimes\rho_{i},\mathcal{O}_{p}\otimes\rho_{j})=\langle\chi_{\rho_{j}},\chi_{\rho_{i}}\rangle-\langle\chi_{\rho_{j}},\chi_{\rho_{i}\otimes\rho_{\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}}}\rangle+\langle\chi_{\rho_{j}},\chi_{\rho_{i}\otimes\rho_{K_{\mathcal{X}}}}\rangle
;

(5)

\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{O}_{p}\otimes\rho_{i})=\langle\chi_{\rho_{i}},\chi_{\rho_{0}}\rangle
;

(6)

\chi(\mathcal{O}_{p}\otimes\rho_{i},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})=\langle\chi_{\rho_{0}},\chi_{\rho_{i}\otimes\rho_{K_{\mathcal{X}}}}\rangle
.

∎

Definition A.6
.

K^{\rm num}(\mathcal{X}):=K_{0}(\mathcal{X})/I
, where
I
is the subgroup of
K_{0}(\mathcal{X})
consisting of those
\kappa_{1}
, which satisfy the condition:
\bar{\chi}(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})=\bar{\chi}(\kappa_{2},\kappa_{1})=0
for all
\kappa_{2}\in K_{0}(\mathcal{X})
. The element of
K^{\rm num}(\mathcal{X})
is called numerical K-class of
\mathcal{X}
.

Lemma A.7
.

The kernel of
\widetilde{\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits}:K_{0}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow H^{\rm even}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C})
is
I
.
∎

Corollary A.8
.

The orbifold chern character map
\widetilde{\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits}:K^{\rm num}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow H^{\rm even}(I\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C})
is an injective map.
∎

Appendix B
Hodge Index Theorem for orbifold surfaces

We recall some basic facts on the de Rham and Hodge theory of projective orbifolds
(i.e. smooth projective Deligne–Mumford stacks with trivial generic stabilizers);
see
[
Ba56
,
Ba57
,
St77
,
Beh04
]
.
Let
\Theta
be a Kähler metric on
\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}
representing
c_{1}
of an ample line bundle. Then:

(i)

H^{m}(\mathcal{X},\underline{\mathbb{C}})=H^{m}(X,\underline{\mathbb{C}})
.

(ii)

H^{m}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an},\underline{\mathbb{Z}})\otimes\mathbb{C}=H^{m}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an},\underline{\mathbb{C}})=H^{m}_{DR}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})\otimes\mathbb{C}
.

(iii)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{p,q}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})<\infty
.

(iv)

(Dolbeault–Kodaira)
H^{q}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an},\Omega^{p}_{\mathcal{X}})=H^{p,q}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})
.

(v)

(Hodge decomposition)

H^{m}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an},\underline{\mathbb{C}})=\bigoplus_{p+q=m}H^{p,q}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}),\qquad H^{p,q}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})=\overline{H^{q,p}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})}.

(vi)

(Hard Lefschetz)

L^{k}:H^{n-k}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an},\underline{\mathbb{C}})\to H^{n+k}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an},\underline{\mathbb{C}})

is an isomorphism.
Define

P^{n-k}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})=\ker\!\left(L^{k+1}:H^{n-k}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an},\underline{\mathbb{C}})\to H^{n+k+2}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an},\underline{\mathbb{C}})\right),

then

H^{m}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an},\underline{\mathbb{C}})=\bigoplus_{k}L^{k}P^{m-2k}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an}).

(vii)

(Hodge–Riemann) The form

Q(\xi,\eta)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\xi\wedge\eta\wedge\Theta^{k}

is positive definite on
P^{p,q}
up to the usual sign convention.

Proposition B.1

(Hodge Index Theorem)
.

For a smooth projective orbifold surface
\mathcal{X}
,

\mathop{\rm NS}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{X})=(P^{1,1}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an})\cap H^{2}(\mathcal{X}^{\rm an},\underline{\mathbb{R}}))\oplus\mathbb{R}\cdot\Theta,

and
Q
restricts to a nondegenerate form of signature
(1,\rho(\mathcal{X})-1)
.
∎

Appendix C
Base change theorem for relative Ext-sheaves on DM stacks

Let
\mathcal{X}
be equipped with the small étale site, and denote the category of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
-modules by
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathcal{X})
. Lemma
C.1
is standard (see
[
Ol16
]
). The proofs of Lemmas
C.3
,
C.5
,
C.6
and Corollaries
C.4
,
C.7
are analogous to the scheme case and are omitted.

Lemma C.1
.

Mod(
\mathcal{X}
) has enough injective objects.

For a morphism of Deligne-Mumford stacks
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
, we have the adjoint functors

f^{*}:\mathrm{Mod}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathrm{Mod}(\mathcal{X}),\quad f_{*}:\mathrm{Mod}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathrm{Mod}(\mathcal{Y}).

For
F\in\mathrm{Mod}(\mathcal{X})
, the functor
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}(F,-)
is left exact, and so is
f_{*}\circ\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}(F,-)
.

Definition C.2
.

\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{f}^{i}(F,-)
is defined to be the
i
-th right derived functor of
f_{*}\circ\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}(F,-)
.

Lemma C.3
.

For
F,G\in\text{Mod}(\mathcal{X})
,
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{f}^{i}(F,G)
is the sheaf associated to the presheaf:

(U\overset{\alpha}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{Y})\mapsto\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{i}(F|_{\mathcal{X}_{U}},G|_{\mathcal{X}_{U}}),

where
U\overset{\alpha}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{Y}
is an étale morphsim from a scheme
U
to
\mathcal{X}
.
∎

Corollary C.4
.

If
F,G
are two quasi-coherent sheaves on
\mathcal{X}
, then
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{f}^{i}(F,G)
are quasi-coherent on
\mathcal{Y}
.
∎

Lemma C.5
.

Assume that
L\in\text{Mod}(\mathcal{X})
and
N\in\text{Mod}(\mathcal{Y})
are locally free sheaves. Then, we have

\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{f}^{i}(L\otimes F,G)=\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{f}^{i}(F,L^{\vee}\otimes G),\quad\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{f}^{i}(F,f^{*}N\otimes G)=\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{f}^{i}(F,G)\otimes N.

∎

Lemma C.6
.

If
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
is an exact sequence in Mod(
\mathcal{X}
), then there is a long exact sequence

∎

Corollary C.7
.

Let
\textstyle{L^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
be an exact sequence in Mod(
\mathcal{X}
). If the complex
L^{\bullet}
satisfies

\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{f}^{i}(L^{e},G)=0\quad\text{for $i\geq 1$},

then we get
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{f}^{i}(F,G)=\mathop{\mathscr{H}}\nolimits^{i}(f_{*}\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}(L^{\bullet},G))\quad\text{for all $i$}
.
∎

Definition C.8
.

A flat family of projective stacks over a
k
-scheme
S
is a flat morphism
p:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow S
, where
\mathcal{X}
is a tame separated
S
-global quotient stack (see
[
EHKV01
, Definition 2.9]
) and
p
factorizes as
\pi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow X
composed with a projective morphism
q:X\rightarrow S
i.e.

\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{S}

Lemma C.9
.

Suppose that
p:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits A
is a flat family of projective stacks over
A
with
A
a finitely generated
k
-algebra. If
E
is a
A
-flat coherent sheaf on
\mathcal{X}
satisfying:

for
i>0
, then for any
A
-algebra
A^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime}
-module
M

\begin{split}p_{A^{\prime}*}(E\otimes_{A}M)=p_{*}E\otimes_{A}M\quad\text{and}\quad R^{i}p_{A^{\prime}*}(E\otimes_{A}M)=0\quad\text{for $i>0$},\end{split}

where

Proof.

Since
\pi_{A^{\prime}}
is exact (
[
AOV08
, Theorem 3.2]
) and
\pi_{A^{\prime}*}
sends injective sheaves to flasque sheaves (
[
Ni08
, Lemma 1.10]
), it suffice to show

(C.1)

\begin{split}q_{A^{\prime}*}(\pi_{A^{\prime}*}(E\otimes_{A}M))&=q_{A^{\prime}*}(\pi_{*}E\otimes_{A}M)=p_{*}E\otimes_{A}M,\\
R^{i}q_{A^{\prime}*}(\pi_{A^{\prime}*}(E\otimes_{A}M))&=R^{i}q_{A^{\prime}*}(\pi_{*}E\otimes_{A}M)=0\end{split}

by
[
Ni08
, Prop. 1.5, Lem. 2.8]
. Since
q:X\rightarrow\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A)
is projective, there exists a closed embedding
\iota:X\rightarrow\mathds{P}^{n}_{A}
i.e.
For the standard affine cover
\mathfrak{U}=(D_{+}(x_{j}))
of
\mathop{\rm Proj}\nolimits(A[x_{0},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}])
, the Čech complex of
E
gives an exact sequence of
A
-modules

(C.2)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise whose terms are flat
A
-modules. Hence, for any
A
-algebra
A^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime}
-module
M
,
H^{0}(X_{A^{\prime}},\pi_{*}E\otimes_{A}M)=H^{0}(X,\pi_{*}E)\otimes_{A}M
and
H^{i}(X_{A^{\prime}},\pi_{*}E\otimes_{A}M)=0
for
i>0
.

∎

Proposition C.10
.

Suppose that
p:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow S
is a flat family of projective stacks over a
k
-scheme
S
of finite type, and let
F,G
be
S
-flat coherent sheaves on
\mathcal{X}
. Then there exists a locally free resolution
\textstyle{L^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
with the universal property: for any morphism of finite type
u:S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
, and any quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}}
-module
M
, one has

\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{p_{S^{\prime}}}^{i}(F\otimes\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}},\,G\otimes M)=\mathop{\mathscr{H}}\nolimits^{i}\!\big(p_{S^{\prime}*}\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(L^{\bullet}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}},\,G\otimes M)\big).

where

\textstyle{X_{S^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{S^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{S^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{S^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{p_{S^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{S}

Proof.

For simplicity, we assume
S=\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A)
. Fix a generating sheaf
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{X}
. The natural morphism

(C.3)

(\pi^{*}\pi_{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\otimes F))\otimes\mathcal{E}\to F

is surjective
[
OS03
]
, and
\pi_{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\otimes F)
is
S
-flat
[
AOV08
, Cor. 3.3]
.
By Serre vanishing, there exists
N
such that
H^{i}(X,\pi_{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\otimes F)(l))=0\quad(i>0,\,l\geq N)
, hence the Mumford-Castelnuovo regularities
{\rm reg}(\pi_{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\otimes F)\otimes_{A}k(s))\leq N+n
for all
s\in S
. Moreover,
q^{*}q_{*}(\pi_{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\otimes F)(l))
is locally free and
q^{*}q_{*}(\pi_{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\otimes F)(l))\rightarrow\pi_{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\otimes F)(l)
is surjective. Writing
V=q_{*}(\pi_{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\otimes F)(l))
, we thus obtain a surjection

(C.4)

\pi^{*}V(-l)\otimes\mathcal{E}\twoheadrightarrow F.

For any
G
, Lemma
C.5
gives

(C.5)

\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{i}_{p}(\pi^{*}V(-l)\otimes\mathcal{E},G)=R^{i}q_{*}(V^{\vee}(l)\otimes\pi_{*}(\mathcal{E}\otimes G)).

As before, there exists
\widetilde{N}
with
{\rm reg}(\pi_{*}(\mathcal{E}\otimes G)\otimes_{A}k(s))\leq\widetilde{N}
, so by
[
Ha77
, Thm. 12.11]
,
R^{i}q_{*}(V^{\vee}(l)\otimes\pi_{*}(\mathcal{E}\otimes G))=0\quad(i>0,\,l\geq\widetilde{N})
.
Thus

(C.6)

\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{i}_{p}(\pi^{*}V(-l)\otimes\mathcal{E},G)=0\quad(i>0,\,l\geq\widetilde{N}).

Iterating (
C.4
), we construct a locally free resolution

(C.7)

L^{\bullet}\to F\to 0

with
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{i}_{p}(L^{e},G)=0
for
i>0
,
e\geq 0
. Hence
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{i}_{p}(F,G)=\mathop{\mathscr{H}}\nolimits^{i}(p_{*}\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(L^{\bullet},G))
. For
S^{\prime}=\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A^{\prime})
, the base change
L^{\bullet}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}
is again a locally free resolution of
F\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}
. By Lemma
C.9
,
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{i}_{p_{S^{\prime}}}(L^{e}\otimes_{A}A^{\prime},\,G\otimes_{A}M)=0\quad(i>0,\,e\geq 0)
, thus (
C.7
) satisfies the required universal property.

∎

Corollary C.11
.

Under the above hypothesis, there exists a complex
W^{\bullet}
of locally free sheaves on
S

such that
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{i}_{p_{S^{\prime}}}(F\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}},G\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}M)=\mathop{\mathscr{H}}\nolimits^{i}(W^{\bullet}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}M)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
C.10
, there exists a bounded below complex
L^{\bullet}
of locally free sheaves such that

(C.8)

\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{p_{S^{\prime}}}^{i}(F\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}},G\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}M)=\mathop{\mathscr{H}}\nolimits^{i}(p_{S^{\prime}*}\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{H}}om}\nolimits(L^{\bullet}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}},G\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}M)).

Since
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits^{i}_{p}(L^{e},G)=R^{i}p_{*}({L^{e}}^{\vee}\otimes G)=0
for
i>0
, each
p_{*}({L^{e}}^{\vee}\otimes G)
is locally free on
S
. Set
W^{\bullet}=p_{*}({L^{\bullet}}^{\vee}\otimes G)
. Then, by (
C.8
) and Lemma
C.9
,
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{p_{S^{\prime}}}^{i}(F\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}},G\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}M)=\mathop{\mathscr{H}}\nolimits^{i}(W^{\bullet}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}M)
.
∎

We state the base change theorem for relative
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits
-sheaves.

Theorem C.12
.

Let
y\in S
be a point.

(1)

If the natural map
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{f}^{i}(F,G)\otimes k(y)\rightarrow\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{i}(F_{y},G_{y})
is surjective, then it is an isomorphism, and the same is true for all
y^{\prime}
in a neighborhood of
y
.

(2)

Assume that the natural map
\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{f}^{i}(F,G)\otimes k(y)\rightarrow\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{i}(F_{y},G_{y})
is surjective. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{f}^{i-1}(F,G)\otimes k(y)\rightarrow\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{i-1}(F_{y},G_{y})
is surjective;

(b)

\mathop{\rm{\mathscr{E}}xt}\nolimits_{f}^{i}(F,G)
is locally free in a neighborhood of
y
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, let
S=\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits(A)
with
A
a finitely generated
k
-algebra.
Define functors
T^{i}:\mathop{\rm Mod}\nolimits(A)\to\mathop{\rm Mod}\nolimits(A)
by

M\mapsto\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{i}(F,G\otimes_{A}M),\quad i\geq 0.

Then the proof of
[
Ha77
, Thm. 12.11]
applies verbatim, yielding the base change theorem for relative Ext-sheaves.
∎
